**Introduction**

This is a book that summarizes the authentic narrated effects transmitted from the noble Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt — Muhammad al-Mukhtar, upon him and upon them be the prayers of the Almighty, the All-Forgiving — compiled at the close of the day and the beginning of the night.

Authored by our master, Qadi al-Nu'man ibn Muhammad ibn Hayyun al-Tamimi — may his intercession be granted and may God forgive him. Amin. O God, Amin.

**On Seeking Sustenance and the General Command to Do So**

Regarding the divine command to seek His bounty — "And seek of His bounty"(1) — it is said: "When the prayer is concluded, spread out in the land and seek the bounty of Allah."(2)

A man came to the Prophet, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, and said: "O Messenger of Allah, my soul has not been content with anything of this world." The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, said to him: "Say: O Allah, please me with Your decree, bless me in what You have given me, and make me content with what You have apportioned for me, so that I may not wish to hasten what You have delayed, nor to delay what You have hastened."

His fathers, upon them be the prayers of Allah, narrated from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, that he said: "The faithful spirit breathed into my soul that a soul shall not die until it has completed its provision. So be at ease in seeking sustenance, for no servant of Allah has a provision between him and it but that patience brings it to him. If he is patient with restraint, it comes to him lawfully, and if he is not patient with restraint, he obtains it unlawfully." He also said, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family: "Nothing will delay any provision of yours, provided that you do not seek it through disobedience to Allah; and be gracious in your asking, for what is with Allah is not attained through disobedience." He further said, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family: "If one of you becomes insolvent, let him go out and seek sustenance, strike the earth, and seek the bounty of Allah. Let him not sit in his house and starve himself and his family while burdening others." He also said, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family: "In the shade of the Throne on the Day when there is no shade but His is a man who went out striking the earth, seeking the bounty of Allah, supporting himself and providing for his children."

A man came and said: "O Messenger of Allah, I have no trade in which I am skilled." He said: "Look for something and take it up once." So the man took up cutting wood. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, then passed by some marketplaces and said to the merchants: "The merchant is licentious, and the licentious are in the Fire." When they heard that, they closed the doors of their shops and abandoned trade. The following day he, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, passed by the market and noticed it deserted. He asked: "What is the matter with them?" It was said: "They abandoned trade when they heard what you said yesterday, O Messenger of Allah."

(1) Al-Nisa': 32
(2) Al-Jumu'ah: 10

<!-- page 10 -->

Volume Two

He said: "I did not mean by that except the one who takes what is not his right and gives it where it does not belong." He said, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family: "I was sent as a mercy to people; I was not sent to be a farmer among them. The worst of this nation are its merchants and its cultivators — except for those who are sparing of themselves for the sake of their religion." He also said, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family: "Verily Allah loves the servant who is easy in selling, easy in buying, easy in settling debts, and easy in what he needs."

A young man with a skin came, driving a camel loaded with goods. It was said to him: "O Messenger, what is the best use of this camel's capacity, and what is the worst?" The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, said to him: "What are you doing with this camel of yours?" He said: "I transport goods and earn from it." He said: "And what do you do with the earnings, tell me?" He said: "I have a wife and children whom I spend on, and I give them from it that they may be independent of others, and I pay off a debt I owe." He, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, said: "If he speaks truthfully, he has a reward like the reward of the one who goes out fighting in the way of Allah, or one performing the Hajj and 'Umrah." He said, may God's prayers be upon him and his family: "What comes to any one of you for the sake of Allah is greater than his striving for his family, that good should come to them." He also said to a man who sought his permission to engage in trade: "Acquire some religious learning first, then the marketplace; for whoever buys and sells without religious learning will fall into usury, and usury here means that which is postponed."

Muhammad ibn al-Munkadir said: "I saw 'Ali ibn al-Husayn, may Allah's peace be upon him, on a very hot day, while he was coming from a village weary and tired. I said to myself: I shall give a word of counsel to a man of his distinction. I said: 'May Allah make you noble — you expose yourself to this in seeking the world; would you see it if your appointed time came to you in this state?' He said: 'If my appointed time came to me while I was in this state, it would come to me while I was in obedience to Allah. I seek to sustain myself from what is lawful so as to suffice for the Hereafter and spare myself from needing others. I was afraid my end might come to me while I was in disobedience to Allah Almighty, and I seek refuge in Allah from that.' He said: So I said to him: 'May I be your ransom — I wished to counsel you, yet you have counseled me.'"

A man said to Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him: "O son of the Messenger of Allah, pray to Allah that He enriches me without need of seeking sustenance." He said: "I will not pray to Allah for that; rather, seek as Allah Almighty has commanded you." He said, may Allah's peace be upon him: "The Muslim should seek his provision until the heat of the sun strikes him." He also said — from the supplications of the Ahl al-Bayt, may Allah's peace be upon them: "O Allah, do not trouble me with a request unless You have decreed it for me, lest my work be prolonged away from obeying You and I be unable to fulfill it. O Allah — and I have not divided

<!-- page 11 -->
*Mukhtasar ul-Asar* — A Concise Collection of Narrated Effects

Volume Two

my affairs — so bring it to me with chastity and ease, and set right for me what You have set right for the righteous." This is reported as the saying of Abu Muhammad ibn 'Ali, may God be pleased with him and have mercy on him, as part of the supplication of Dawud, may Allah's peace be upon him. Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's peace be upon him, said to Sufyan: "Sufyan, if a blessing appears to you, praise Allah, and if your provision is delayed, seek forgiveness from Allah." Then he recited: "Ask forgiveness of your Lord; He is ever Forgiving — He will send rain upon you in abundance, and make rivers for you."(1) He said: "And if something of the trials of this world befalls you, say: 'There is no power except with Allah, the Most High, the Most Great.'" Sufyan said: "Take these three — and what are the three?" He, may Allah's peace be upon him, also said to a man from among his Shi'a, advising him: "If you travel or when you intend to depart for your livelihood, and you desire success in that, perform two rak'ahs. When you complete the tashahhud, say: 'O Allah, I go forth — if You willed morning, I departed; if I remain in my soul, I ask as You have commanded me — grant me from Your bounty a good, lawful provision as You have commanded me, and grant me the wellness that You have given me.' Say that three times. Then perform two more rak'ahs, and after the tashahhud say: 'Tomorrow — if it was morning, or I went — if I was in a soul, by Allah's power and strength, and there is no power from me but with Your power, O Allah, and Your strength is greater. O Allah, to You is the direction of strength. O Allah, I ask You the best of this day if it is a day, or the best of this night if it is a night, and I ask You to grant me a lawful, good provision, leading me to what surrounds You by Your strength, while I am humble, in chastity.' Say that three times, then rise to fulfill your purpose." He, may Allah's peace be upon him, also said: "Allah Almighty has distributed provision among His servants; those who ask Allah for His bounty are the best of them." He said: "The one who goes out seeking lawful provision is like a mujahid for the sake of Allah." He also said to a man from among his Shi'a who intended to engage in trade: "If you seek something of the world and miss it, remember what Allah Almighty has singled you out for from His religion, and what He has given you from what He has distracted others away from — that will make it easier for you to rise above it." 'Ali ibn al-Husayn, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: "Allah Almighty has made provision ten parts: nine of it in trade and one part in other things." A man said to my father, 'Abd Allah Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may God's prayers be upon him, who used to look after him: "O son of the Messenger of Allah, I have abandoned trade, waiting for your command to appear." He said to him: "Do not leave your door, and spread

<!-- page 12 -->

out your carpet, for your provision from Allah is better for you. If you cease seeking sustenance, your wealth will diminish; so seek the bounty of Allah, your Lord."

**On What Is Forbidden to Sell**

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, forbade the sale of free persons and declared it null and void, as well as the sale of that which is unlawfully taken, of alcohol, and of the dead. It is transmitted from the Imams of his family, may Allah's prayers be upon them, that every prohibited thing may neither be sold nor may earnings be derived from it, nor may its price be accepted — such as carrion, blood, swine, wine, musical instruments and all objects of amusement. They forbade engaging in those things, profiting from them, selling them, buying them, learning them, and assisting with them in any way.

However, they permitted the sale of grapes, juice, raisins, honey, dates, and figs, even to one who will make from them wine or intoxicants — for selling these commodities is lawful, though the sin belongs to the one who makes them unlawful. They also permitted the acceptance of prices whenever the sale of something is prohibited to a buyer who intends to use it for himself, though it remains forbidden for that person to do so.

<!-- page 12 -->
As for the price received by the one from whom it has lawfully passed into another's possession — there is no harm in that, nor is there anything wrong with pledging allegiance to polytheists or accepting prices for what they purchase from Muslims. What is in the hands of polytheists that they have sold and taken possession of is theirs. It is not permissible to sell to them what is impermissible to sell, and most of their wealth is from usury. It is taken from them as the jizya and as prices for what they purchase from Muslims, and it is lawful for those who receive it.

They, may Allah's peace be upon them, disliked the price of a dog, though they deemed it permissible to sell a cattle dog, and they disliked the price of a singing girl. They forbade buying what one knows to have been stolen or obtained through betrayal, until it is returned to its rightful owners, and then one may buy from them. They permitted the sale of the Qur'an.

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, forbade in general the sale of water, kidney-fat, and fire. The prohibition on that applies to selling what no one possesses — such as water in the open wilderness, floodwaters, and the like, of waters that no one owns, and the flame of fire such as that which is kindled and spread about. As for one who has a well that he has dug in his possession, or a spring that flows, or a river he has developed, or something manufactured, or rainwater that has settled in his property and land, or in a vessel that he has stored — whether he carries it on his back, his horse, or by whatever means he transports it — then it is permissible for him to sell it.

Similarly, if pasture grass is taken from the wild, or uprooted and cultivated, or watered and grown in land that he owns, it is permissible for him to sell it. As for fire — whoever owns it


<!-- page 13 -->
Such as firewood, charcoal, or any other thing that burns, it is permissible to sell it, because it constitutes wealth. There is nothing wrong with buying and selling from those who are known for treachery, theft, and other wrongdoing, unless one knows that what is being purchased from them is itself stolen or acquired through treachery. If one does not know that, then there is nothing wrong with such a transaction. We have mentioned that buying and selling from polytheists is permissible when one does not know that they have acted treacherously or through theft, though a Muslim is expected to investigate the matter.

**Mentioning the Prohibition Against Selling Gharar:**

Allah Almighty said: "And do not consume one another's wealth unjustly." The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and his family), as narrated through the pure Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt (peace be upon them), prohibited all forms of gharar sales. A gharar sale is every sale concluded over something unknown. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and his family) explained several aspects of this, including his prohibition of the sale of the rope of a rope. Among such sales is a type that people of the pre-Islamic era practiced: selling a commodity in proportion to the price of what a camel produces, including selling its produce before it has been produced. They used to pledge allegiance over lambs still in the bellies of camels and other animals. It was also forbidden (may Allah bless him and his family) to sell what is contained in the womb and what issues from stallions — that is, the embryos in the bellies of their mothers — and the output of a stallion that has not yet occurred. They used to bind themselves over what a stallion would produce for a given period, whether once or several times. Among the prohibitions (may Allah bless him and his family) is the sale of touch: when a man sells a garment or something folded that cannot be inspected, or makes it such that it is only touched by hand without the option to examine it upon viewing or unfolding, or says that if he touches the commodity it becomes obligatory for it to be sold between them — for a contract of sale is only binding upon stated agreement and parting with mutual consent. Also included is touching goods from behind a cover, so that the sale is based on this alone. Among the prohibitions (may Allah bless him and his family) is the sale of al-munabadhah: when a man throws a folded garment to another and says, "I have bought this from you, and if it is returned to me, the sale is concluded between us with no right of withdrawal for either of us," or when each of them gives a garment or something to its owner,

<!-- page 14 -->
pledging over it, saying this without turning it over or looking at it. Among the prohibitions (may Allah bless him and his family) is the sale of pebbles — a type of sale practiced in the pre-Islamic era in which the contract between two parties was concluded by each throwing his share, making that throw the binding act of sale. He (may Allah bless him and his family) thereby nullified all these sales and prohibited the sale of al-wala' and its gift, saying that al-wala' is only for those who give it by lineage and that it is not to be sold. He prohibited the sale of a slave on the condition of keeping him before he comes into possession, as well as the sale of a stray camel and similar animals. Al-Sadiq Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said: If such a stray animal or its like is sold together with something present — such as a slave, a bear, a garment, or any other permissible item that is present — then the sale of that combination is permissible, provided that the seller and buyer both know the stray animal or absent item. The price, upon its arrival, falls upon the present item that the sellers have seen. This does not fall under the words of Allah Almighty, "And do not consume one another's wealth unjustly."

He (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said: Similarly, if one sells the milk in the udder of his sheep along with a quantity already drawn from it that is permissible to sell, this is permissible, because the price falls on the present item that the sellers have seen. Most sales also fall on what the sellers have seen, or what some of them have seen and purchased while the greater part is concealed — as in doors, quarters, garments, animals, and the like — where one sees what is apparent but not what is hidden, and the sale covers both the visible and the hidden. It is permissible to buy metal dust with gold and silver hand to hand, even if upon extraction it yields more or less than what was purchased, because at the time of purchase it was neither silver nor gold but merely dust. It is disliked to sell it by lineage. It is narrated from 'Ali (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) that he disliked the sale of fish still in the water when it can only be reached by fishing — so if there is no one to hunt it, hunting and selling it is permissible. It is forbidden to sell wool on the backs of sheep and their skins, as well as

<!-- page 15 -->
to sell a man's share of the spoils before he receives it, because he does not know what will come to him from it. The sale of sukuk — that is, written certificates of debt — is also prohibited.

**Mention of the Sale of Fruits:**

The Imams from the household of the Prophet narrated that he forbade the sale of fruits in general until their goodness becomes apparent. Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said that the ripening of fruits begins when they turn red or yellow. He meant (may Allah bless him and his family) that if something of the fruit becomes good, it is permissible to sell all of them. If none has yet ripened, the sale is to be conducted on the basis of the original principle he mentioned regarding the sale of present and absent goods. If nothing of the fruit has appeared that is free from what is objectionable, the sale is not permissible unless both parties agree to it, and if either of them annuls it, it is annulled — even if the types differ from all other kinds and nothing of it deviates from the original. If the aforementioned fruit is sold in a single transaction, and they say to one another: if the fruit blooms, or some of it sells, and their trees do not produce these fruits for a year or two after that — and it was mentioned that if no produce comes in the future, the price falls on what was present at the time of sale — this too is based on the aforementioned principle.

The general public does not allow it to be sold for years, claiming that it is deceptive, yet they allow it to be sold once some of it shows goodness. What is pursued by them is not consistent, and the goodness that appears while the sale encompasses the remainder falls under the same ruling — and this is what they denied and rejected, because they denied that part of the sale could take place before its goodness had commenced, while still allowing its sale once some goodness had appeared. The price in that case lies in what has appeared. Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said: It is permissible to sell the fruit if the goodness of it or of some of it has commenced for a year or two after that, even if it does not come out in this year but comes out in the next, and in this they looked to what we mentioned regarding the sale. Some of what was said contrary to this from the general public notwithstanding, it is their duty to follow the saying of the Imams (peace be upon them) and leave dispute with them. What has come from them (may Allah's prayers be upon them) is an argument that whoever holds it is dispensed from seeking further evidence. The most important matter regarding the sale of fruit is a comprehensive hadith that has been narrated for many years. They claimed it was narrated from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and his family)

<!-- page 16 -->
that he forbade the sale of years, without mentioning fruit or anything else. This hadith, if proven, is a comprehensive hadith, and the interpretation of the Imams (may Allah's prayers be upon them) shows that what is meant is not what they oblige upon it. If it is general, ruling by it in all that falls under its scope is required, which itself necessitates many considerations, including the fact that in the Arabic language "years" refers to times of hardship and adversity. The Arabs say "the years have come upon us," meaning adversities and tribulations — and this is "the year of the year," meaning the year of drought and hardship. On this view, it becomes necessary to forbid what is sold in adversity, such that every sale conducted during it is invalid. It is also possible that in the metaphorical usage of the language, the sale of years means selling by the year and for years, which itself requires many considerations. Following what has been said in the explanation of the Imams (may Allah's prayers be upon them) takes precedence, especially what the foundational principles testify to and upon which the subsidiary rulings are built. Whoever sells fruit that has not yet appeared, and the buyer accepts it, and neither of them cancels the sale but proceeds with it — then it is permissible and not forbidden, on account of what has been narrated regarding its prohibition.

As Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said: The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and his family) forbade the sale of fruits before their goodness appeared, because they used to purchase them while they might perish, and disputes used to arise with the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and his family) over this before completion and soundness. When these disputes increased, he forbade them from selling until the goodness became clear. He forbade it for them, and said: Whoever sells his fruit and excludes something known from it, that sale is permissible. Whoever sells a sale excluding something unknown, that sale is not permissible, because the contract was made over something unknown. He said: If the buyer sells the fruit before he harvests it, that sale is permissible.

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and his family) forbade the sale of dates on the heads of the palm tree for dates, and grapes in the vineyard for raisins, using a measure. He said it is not permissible to sell grain before the higher authority is able to harvest it in the customary manner. If it is sold for wheat, or on the condition that it be left, and the buyer harvests it or leaves it until it is dispersed, then there is nothing wrong with it. Thus it is permissible to sell crops of sowing and wet crops if that is known, purchasing it as a harvest — and the original asset remains for the seller if the harvest expires.

<!-- page 17 -->
The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and his family) said: Whoever buys a palm tree with fruit already in it that has been mentioned — meaning its fruit is given to the seller — unless the buyer stipulates that it belongs to him. This includes the ruling on the sale of trees with fruits in them: the fruit of the palm tree does not pass to the buyer, while the wells, if the buyer does not stipulate them, belong to the seller.

**Mentioning the Prohibition Against Cheating and Deception:**

The Imams from the Ahl al-Bayt of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and his family) narrated that he said: "Whoever cheats us is not one of us," and that he forbade cheating, treachery, deception, and deceit in selling and repudiation. He commanded the fulfillment of contracts in matters of sale, purchase, marriage, and covenants. The words of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and his family), "Whoever cheats us is not one of us," mean that such a person is not among those who follow us in our command and are of our ruling — not that whoever cheats thereby leaves the religion of Islam and apostatizes as an unbeliever, even though that too carries its own obligation regarding the one who apostatizes from Islam. Allah Almighty said, in the story of Ibrahim (peace be upon him and his family): "Whoever follows me is of me, and whoever disobeys me — then You are indeed Forgiving and Merciful." The one who cheats did not follow the command of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and his family), so he is not among those who followed his command. Yet if he was a Muslim, he is among some of those who followed him but disobeyed him in some of what he commanded, and his matter is referred to Allah Almighty — if He wills, He forgives him, and if He wills, He punishes him — like the ruling of other disobedient Muslims. Al-Sadiq Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said: Mixing two types of food and the like — good and bad — if done with the intention of selling constitutes cheating. He (may Allah's prayers be upon him) meant that if one shows the buyer the good and conceals the inferior, such that either both appear together or only the good appears while the inferior is hidden, then this is cheating. He (may Allah's prayers be upon him) used to go around the markets of Kufa warning against cheating and saying: Show from my side what you sell of goodness. He (may Allah's prayers be upon him) forbade blowing into meat and said: "Blowing is from the devil, so do not blow into food or drink." He permitted (may Allah's prayers be upon him) blowing to inflate the skin between the hide and the meat, but forbade blowing into the flesh because of what we have mentioned — that it constitutes cheating, as such blowing enters the sinews and thin layers beneath the skin, making it appear fat when it is not. He forbade boiling milk with water for those who wish to sell it, though if one wishes to drink it


<!-- page 18 -->

There is nothing wrong with that. The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) forbade tafif — shortchanging in measure and balance, and reduction in measure and weight beyond what is excess when giving. It is more proper to give to the one who sells or takes from another, and the one who takes should give fully and do justice in it. Allah Almighty said: "Woe to the mutafifeen — those who, when they take measure from people, take in full, and when they give by measure or by weight, give less." That is, if they take from them, they give them that in full; but this is in the case of selling, weighing, or giving by measure.

As for reward and voluntary charity, whoever gives in full receives a greater reward. If he falls short in voluntary charity, there is no liability upon him; but if it is obligatory, he must fulfill it and be reminded of the proper measure when paying it. The deception here is that one increases weight when taking and decreases it when giving, as we have mentioned. It is obligatory to observe the commandment that Allah Almighty forbade and warned against in His Book. Ali (peace be upon him) commanded vigilance in the buying and selling of dinars and dirhams. Nothing unlawful entered his house from it, and this was something obscure that was scarcely known except to the people of Basra, or through ignorance and others. Either it was not widely known, or it was hardly concealed from the people and was taken at its proper price, or it was a known currency that people exchanged at increased value — in which case there is nothing wrong with buying and selling with it, along with pure copper and other things that are known and not unknown. It was narrated from Ja'far ibn Muhammad that the prayers of Allah be upon him — he forbade the difference of weights in the same region because of the suspicion and confusion involved in that. The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) also forbade tasriyah — that those who wish to sell a sheep or a milking camel leave it without milking for a day or two so that milk collects in its udder, then sell it in that condition, so that the buyer sees a large udder and desires it. The buyer is thereby deceived and harmed by this. He has three days — that is, after milking it — within which, if he wishes, he may keep it, or if he wishes, he may return it along with a sa' of dates to compensate for the milk he obtained from it. If he found less milk in it upon return, nothing further is owed, and this option is the option regarding the animal: the Musarrat is to be returned even if

<!-- page 19 -->

he renounces the option of three days, provided that he conceals the defect while knowing of it before the sale and points it out to the buyer, and informs him how many days milking was withheld. He would not then be required to return it with harm, except that he finds a defect in it other than that. The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) forbade najsh in transactions. He said: "Do not practice najsh and do not outbid one another," for those who engage in najsh sow discord, enmity, harshness, and estrangement — as though he meant that none of you should interfere in his brother's transaction so as to cause estrangement and the like. Najsh is when someone who has no intention of buying approaches a commodity and raises its price without wishing to purchase it, but does so for others to hear and raise their bids further, intending thereby to create a false impression. This is a form of deception, because the excess bid entices others to exceed it further, and therefore it is forbidden.

The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) forbade a townsman selling on behalf of a Bedouin. This means: when a Bedouin brings a commodity and hands it to a townsman to sell it on his behalf, and the townsman sells it without allowing it to be offered for sale in the open market — this prohibition is well understood. Either the townsman is to sell it for him as instructed, or he is to call it out and let it be known at what price it stands, so the buyer is informed and the owner is then told what was offered; then he may order it sold or come and sell it himself. This latter case carries no prohibition, because a command attributed to an act is assigned to the one who commanded it. As they said: the Messenger of Allah (peace be upon him) cut off the hand of a thief, stoned an adulterer who was fortified by marriage, and ordered the killing of those who were obligated to be killed — yet all of this was done by his command (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him). Similarly they say that the prince killed so-and-so or struck so-and-so, even if it was another who carried out the act by his order. So if the Bedouin instructs a townsman to sell his goods, it is the Bedouin who has effectively sold them, even if al-Hadari or someone else executed the sale without having been ordered to do so and without the owner's permission — in that case, the sale is not permissible unless the owner of the commodity authorizes it, and this is what is forbidden.

The Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) forbade receiving the rukban — that is, going out to meet the caravans and merchants arriving with goods outside the city or the marketplace where they intend to sell, out of fear of wronging them and of excluding those who were unable to go out to that place to buy from them. It was also a form of deception because the one who goes out to receive them knows the prices and market values of which the sellers are unaware. Al-Sadiq Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said: whoever goes out to receive goods on a day's march or less

<!-- page 20 -->

— if it is more than that, he should not receive them; and if the goods are of good quality, the more he receives them, the more it benefits the buyer — it is cheating and is forbidden for him. Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's prayers be upon him) was also asked about a man who buys food and then sells the same measure of it in return. He said: "If that does not increase it, then it is permissible; but if he uses it to deceive the Muslims, then there is no good in it and it does not make it sound." He was also asked about a man who buys food by weight and finds more than he paid for in it. He replied: "If this is a virtue that people commonly practice and the like of it is permissible and occurs between sellers, then it is fine. But if it is blameworthy, it is wrong and must be returned to the seller unless he is himself pleased with it." From Ali (may Allah's prayers be upon him), he permitted the buyer to increase the seller's weight or amount of money beyond his due, after the buyer has received his full right — this being a gift from the buyer. The seller thereby repents of his action and makes amends to the buyer. But if he does not repent of it, the buyer is not required to hate him for it. However, if the buyer stipulates this as a condition in the purchase contract and it is unknown, the purchase is null and void; if it was known, it is not invalidated, and the known increase within a contract of measure, weight, and justice in such a case is nothing other than what is true — whether in excess or in deficit — which is the fulfillment that Allah Almighty commanded: "Weigh with the qistas," and He says — and He is the most truthful of speakers — "Give full measure and do not be among those who cause loss. Weigh with a straight balance and do not defraud people of their things, and do not make mischief in the land." Justice and truth are neither increase nor decrease; underestimation is shortage, and diminution is increase — for those who, when they take from people in measure, take in full, but when they give by measure or weight, they cause loss.

Mention of What He Forbade Regarding Sales:

The Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon them) narrated that the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) forbade two conditions in a single sale — that is, saying: "I will sell you this commodity for cash at such a price, and on credit for such a price,"

<!-- page 21 -->

with the choice being his. If he pays the lesser amount in cash or the greater for a deferred payment, the sale is not permissible because it is indeterminate. It is also not permissible to sell something for dinars on the condition that he receive from him dirhams in their place at the time of exchange, nor to sell on the condition that the other party sell him something else in a guaranteed sale — whether sooner or later — nor to sell for a fixed term and increase the price if the term is extended. All of this and its like constitutes two conditions in a single sale, which is a corrupt sale. However, if the two parties contract it and then separate from it by mutual agreement and settle on one of the two sales, the other being annulled, and they part on that basis, it is permissible and the sale is whatever they agreed upon.

It is also narrated that the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) forbade profiting from something that is not yet guaranteed — that is, something that has been weighed or measured, for which the seller bears no counter-liability, or a livelihood paid for before it is received, so that he sells it before taking possession of it. Similarly, a craftsman who accepts work for a wage and then accepts goods in lieu of less than what he contracted for and prefers one arrangement over another does not thereby mean the original contract is invalidated. If he fixes something in it that he has already worked on, he may take credit for that, and from that he may have a quarter added to what he charges. He and the other party have the option to agree to as much as he has accumulated or to settle within it in part, even if they calculate a quarter of the payments, review part of them, or act in accordance with what is good for him. The credit is like a khan that he opens, hangs the curtain for, sweeps, manages his affairs in, and charges for its use — all of which is permissible and there is nothing wrong with it.

It is also narrated that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) forbade a sale combined with a loan — that is, a man saying to another man: "I will sell you such-and-such on the condition that you lend me such-and-such," or "Lend me a loan as though I were obligated to do so." If the sale contract is concluded on this basis, it is invalid; and if the borrower does as stated above, the sale is invalidated — though the unconditional loan within the sale contract is not invalidated. It is narrated that the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) also forbade the sale of what one does not possess — such as a man bargaining over something he does not own. The sale is attributed to him, and the buyer has the option upon seeing it. From that, he may sell a guaranteed item for a future time when no such item exists — such as grapes or fruit in a season when none are available — and this is the prohibition of kali for kali, meaning debt for debt, which is not permissible. This is like a man who hands over to another man a sum of dinars in exchange for a specified quantity of food to be delivered at a set time, and when the time comes, the food is not found, so he purchases it

<!-- page 22 -->

from someone who owes him a debt to be paid at a later date, and gives that food on the condition that the price be deferred as well — this too is not permissible, because it is a debt within a debt. Or a man has a debt that has come due, and they agree to extend the term in exchange for increasing the debt — this is not permissible. However, if he is allowed to pay it off early before the deadline, there is nothing wrong with that, because if he leaves off everything good for his counterpart, he has done well. It is also said that if the one owed a debt does something for the debtor by mutual agreement, this is invalid. The worker has a similar right to a comparable wage if one or both of them does so, and he returns the credit or takes what is left. If the debt has a set term and it is paid off before the term arrives, that is fine.

One of the cases is that he takes one denomination and transfers it to another — this is not permissible. Similarly, if a debt is owed to a group and they divide it on the condition that each of them is entitled to whatever portion falls to him — this is not permissible unless each of them takes only what is between them in actuality. What falls within this meaning and enters into the category of kali for kali: if two men each owe the other a debt — whether in food or in anything else — the amount of which is unknown, on condition that each of them releases his companion from what he owes, there is nothing wrong with that, because it is a matter of what is unknown and not yet determined, and it is not from the category of kali for kali. They also permitted the sale of animals to one another for more than their count, and it is narrated that he (may Allah's prayers be upon him) sold a camel worth twenty camels for twenty camels to be delivered at a future time — this is permissible if the camels are described. They forbade the sale of meat for a living animal, and also forbade the sale of meat for the money of its equivalent. This means that if a seller proceeds to a sale and there is bidding in the transaction and it is a straightforward exchange, there is nothing wrong with that. The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) sold in the case of those who increase some of what they were ordered to sell, and permitted taking possession of food before it is caught in sale of fruits on the tops of trees, and in the sale of food by the seller when the buyer knows of it and accepts it. He also forbade the monopolizing of food, and it is narrated that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) was emphatic about this and stern regarding it. Al-Sadiq Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said: the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) was harsh in his argument against Hakim ibn Hizam, a man from the Quraysh who possessed great wealth, and when food entered Madinah


<!-- page 23 -->
A brief book on narrated reports, Volume Two. A man buys food in large quantities and the people raise its price — he trades in it, and that is fine, so long as the people can find what to buy from him. If they cannot, he has no right to monopolize it against them and let them perish. He must bring it out and sell it, and he is forbidden from selling at a higher price than the going rate of that place. He will be told: either sell at the price the people sell at, or remove your goods from their market, so that inflated prices do not afflict them.

He was asked about an unjust ruler who seizes a man, imposes a financial penalty upon him, and compels him to sell his property — is it permissible for the buyer to return it to him afterwards? He said: No, the buyer bears no obligation, but the benefit has come to him through the harm inflicted on the seller, because he bought from someone who was not freely willing to sell. If the seller was not compelled to sell, and the Sultan acts unjustly, then the Sultan is obliged to make restitution to him. However, the sale of one who is compelled to sell must be annulled if he was forced to sell by someone who had power over him — even if it was only an unjust Sultan who had compelled him to sell, and the seller feared punishment if he refused. As for one who sold to a party other than the one compelling him — one who neither coerced him nor forced him to sell to them specifically — the sale is permissible and irrevocable.

It is reported that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family) commanded a man to sell at a price comparable to its equivalent. This was a matter of discipline, for it has been said that if it were sold, the set price would not adequately reflect its worth. Likewise, it is confirmed that he (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) forbade buying and selling in the mosque, at the time of Friday prayer, and before sunrise. Al-Sadiq Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's blessings be upon him) said: The second call to prayer on Friday — that is, the call which is made while the Imam is upon the pulpit — is the one that prohibits buying and selling until the Friday prayer has been completed.

**On the Disbursement:**

Allah Almighty said: *"O you who believe, do not devour riba in multiplied amounts, and fear Allah, that you may succeed."*

The Imams from the Ahl al-Bayt of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family) narrated that he cursed the one who consumes riba, his agent, his seller, his buyer, his scribe, and his witnesses. He said that the one who consumes riba will be raised on the Day of Resurrection in a state of madness. The Commander of the Believers said — and Allah's prayers be upon him —

<!-- page 24 -->
— that riba is one of the major sins. Abu Abdullah al-Sadiq Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's blessings be upon him) narrated from the saying of Allah Almighty that Allah destroys usury and raises charity. It was said to him: We have seen some people who practise riba accumulate great wealth. He said: And what ultimate right is more deserving of ruin than a dirham of riba? A man who repents of it as an act of returning from wrongdoing to his family, yet nothing remains to him — and if he is imprisoned and does not repent from it, he will enter the Fire.

They said — peace be upon them — that it is forbidden to permit the people of dhimmah to practise riba openly. Muhammad ibn Ali (may Allah's blessings be upon him) narrated that the Messenger of Allah (peace be upon him and his family) did not accept the jizyah from the dhimmis unless it was stipulated that they should not consume riba or engage in it, nor eat pork, nor contract marriage with sisters, nieces, or similar forbidden relations. Whoever among them did so today faces no reproach, but most of those who say otherwise have taken what was forbidden to them and devoured it, consuming that which was forbidden to them and turning many from the path of Allah. Their mother is a great one and they are accustomed to the painful punishment of its palms. Usury is forbidden to them in their own Shari'ah, and if they permit it for themselves — riba in the language means increase; it is said that the wealth of a man grows, meaning it increases, and its owner causes it to grow if it increases — then from this it is called what rises from the earth, increasing beyond what surrounds it from the land. An increase in wealth from a lawful direction, even if it takes this form, is not included in the meaning of riba that is forbidden. Rather, riba is forbidden as narrated by the Imams (may Allah bless them and grant them peace) in what the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) ruled: it is only permissible in exchange of like for like, hand to hand, with no increase or decrease, on either side.

The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) said: Gold for gold, silver for silver, hand to hand, and weight for weight — whoever increases or accepts an increase has engaged in riba. Wheat for wheat, barley for barley, flour for flour, dates for dates, raisins for raisins, corn for corn, basket for basket, rice for rice — whoever increases or accepts an increase has engaged in riba. Muhammad ibn Ali (may Allah's blessings be upon him) said: Gold may not be exchanged for gold, nor silver for silver, with any increase in weight. Abu Abdullah Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may

<!-- page 25 -->
Allah's blessings be upon him) said: It is not permissible to add to that, even if one of the two items is of better quality than the other. That is, if one sells gold for gold, or silver for silver, or anything that is weighed or measured for its like, this is only permissible at weight for weight — even if it is from among weighed goods, or by weight against measure if it falls among what is weighed — or a vessel of the finest gold purchased with lesser gold, which is only permissible at weight for weight, so long as the name of gold applies to both. This applies until it becomes pure copper, predominantly copper and attributed to copper, upon which the name of gold no longer applies, and he may then buy it with gold as one buys copper, with differentiation. The same applies to silver for silver in this sense, and likewise to everything that is weighed or measured.

If the two types differ, al-Sadiq Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's blessings be upon him) said accordingly: When selling gold for gold and silver for silver, where one of them resembles a decorated sword or a ring and its like, he sells it for something of the other type — whether gold for silver or silver for gold — at the higher value, until it is known that its price exceeds that amount, so gold is for gold and silver for silver, weight for weight, with the remainder of the price being in the sword, ring, or whatever it may be. He also said: It is permissible to take dirhams from dinars and dinars from dirhams — meaning, may Allah bless him and his family — when a man has dinars owed to him in advance, or from a sale or any rightful claim, and he takes dirhams from it at their value or what they agree upon; or he has dirhams owed to him and takes dinars from them. Likewise in reverse. He said: It is not permissible to exchange except hand to hand. If one buys dinars for dirhams, dirhams for dinars, gold for silver, silver for gold, or any part of that, he must not leave until he has taken delivery. If the other party says, "Send a messenger and he will fulfil it for you," do not accept that. Rather, if he says give and take, or stay with him, do not leave him. If you send your messenger with him, instruct your messenger to take delivery from him on the spot before departing; if he leaves before taking delivery or you do so, the exchange between you is void. He said: Whoever advances dirhams or dinars better than what is owed — there is nothing wrong with that, provided it is in the interest of the borrower and it is not stipulated as a condition at the time of lending.

<!-- page 26 -->
**On the Sale of Food to One Another:**

All the pure Imams from the family of the Messenger of Allah (peace be upon him) narrated that he forbade differentiation between items of the same kind where they are weighed or measured — and this has been mentioned above. Whoever buys something from its same kind, where differentiation is not permissible between them, may not differentiate. If the two kinds differ, differentiation is permissible between them, just as it is permissible between silver, gold, iron, and similar goods whose kinds differ, if they fall among what is weighed or measured. Al-Sadiq Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's blessings be upon him) said accordingly: It is not permissible to sell a sa' of almonds for two sa's of the same, nor a sa' of dates for two sa's of the same, but it is permissible to sell a sa' of wheat for two sa's of dates, and a sa' of dates for two sa's of raisins. The same applies to foodstuffs and fruits of the land — they are of one category; if the two kinds differ, there is nothing wrong with differentiation, hand to hand, and that is the sound view. He said: Barley and wheat are of one kind and it is not permissible to differentiate between them. Likewise, wheat, flour, and suede — these are only permissible in equal measure. When it was said to him (peace be upon him): Does it have virtue within it, meaning in the grain? He said: Does it not have provisions — meaning flour and the like? He said: No. The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) forbade the sale of dates in their fresh, wet state because when they dry they will diminish in weight. He (peace be upon him) permitted the sale of garments for one another, one for more than one. He said — peace be upon him: The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) forbade the sale of food to one another arbitrarily and without effort, but permitted the exchange of bread for flour, and vinegar for vinegar, even if their kinds differ — and sugar for honey from bees, for example — hand to hand.

**On the Option of the Two Parties to a Sale:**

The pure Imams from the household of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family) narrated that the two parties of a transaction retain the option to withdraw until they separate by mutual agreement. Al-Sadiq Ja'far ibn Muhammad (peace be upon him) said: Separation means the physical parting from the place where they concluded the sale. He said: Abu Radwan (may Allah be pleased with him) bought land, and when he needed to confirm the transaction he stood up, walked a short distance, and then returned, intending thereby to make the sale binding. He said — peace be upon him: The Messenger of Allah (peace be upon him) said: Muslims are bound by their stipulated conditions, except for a condition that contradicts the Book of Allah the Almighty.

<!-- page 27 -->
A woman from a nation of people had a document of emancipation, and some of the wives of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) came to assist her with her emancipation document. She said: I will give you what you need and free you. Her guardians said: We will not sell her to one who frees her unless her loyalty remains with us. This was reported to the Messenger of Allah (peace be upon him and his family), and he said: Buy her and free her, for loyalty belongs to the one who frees. Then he addressed the people and said: What is the matter with people who stipulate conditions not found in the Book of God? Every condition contrary to the Book of God is rejected. Whoever sells something and stipulates in his contract a condition that is permissible, it is permissible. If he stipulates what is not permissible, the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) did not permit it — he did not permit their stipulation of loyalty to themselves, because he said (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) that loyalty belongs to the one who frees.

People differed regarding a sale with a condition. Some said: Whoever sells something and stipulates a condition in his contract, the condition is void. They argued this on the basis of what was narrated in general from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family): that he forbade a sale with a condition. Others said: Both the sale and the condition are permissible, citing the fact that the Messenger of Allah (peace be upon him) bought a camel from Jabir ibn Abdullah al-Ansari during one of his expeditions with the condition that Jabir would ride it to Madinah. Others said: The sale is permissible but the condition is void, citing the hadith of Barirah which we have mentioned. The statement that came from the People of the House — Allah's blessings be upon them — resolves all of this, proves the correct position, and clarifies it. The voiding of the condition stipulated by the people dealing with Barirah was because they stipulated what is not permissible — since the Messenger of Allah (peace be upon him) had ruled that loyalty belongs to the one who frees, and they stipulated loyalty to themselves apart from the one who frees. As for the condition of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) and his family, namely Jabir ibn Abdullah's riding the camel purchased from him — this was a permissible condition, not one that makes lawful what is forbidden or renders lawful what is prohibited. As for what is narrated about his forbidding the sale with a condition — if it is authentic — it is a general statement whose intended meaning is a condition that is not permissible. The Ahl al-Bayt (peace and blessings of Allah be upon them) therefore said: A sale with a condition in it is valid if the condition is permissible, and it is binding. If the condition is not permissible, it is neither to be honoured nor enforced, though the sale itself is permissible in both instances. Al-Sadiq Ja'far ibn Muhammad (peace be upon him) said: If a man sells a slave woman to another man and stipulates in the contract of sale that he shall not sell her, nor give her away, nor shall she inherit from him if he dies — then the conditions of not selling and not giving away

<!-- page 28 -->


<!-- page 28 -->
He is obliged to fulfill it because he has agreed to it, while the condition that the slave girl does not inherit from him is not obligatory, because Allah Almighty has ordained inheritance for a man's family. Therefore, a condition that excludes them is not permissible, and the position of the Ahl al-Bayt, peace be upon them, differs from the general opinion on this matter. Al-Sadiq Ja'far ibn Muhammad, peace be upon him, said that whoever buys something with the right of choice for a fixed period, and the item perishes before the buyer exercises that choice, the loss falls upon the seller's wealth, and the buyer must swear that he did not make his choice. If he refuses to swear, the sale becomes binding upon him. He also said, peace be upon him, that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's prayers be upon him and his family, granted the buyer of an animal the option of three days, whether or not this was stipulated in the contract, and that there is no option for the seller once the two parties have separated. If the buyer brings about a new circumstance before the three days are up, the sale becomes binding upon him. Among such circumstances is if his slave girl touches her, kisses her, or looks upon what was forbidden to him before the purchase, or if he rides the animal, or if he offers the commodity for sale — in the latter case, he must swear that he did not intend to sell it but merely wished to inspect it before deciding whether to keep or return it. He also said that if the buyer sells what he purchased while the option was still in force, and there is a witness testifying before the sale that he had chosen it and was satisfied with it, then the price belongs to him even without such testimony; but if he does not swear to it and refuses the oath, the right reverts to the seller. He said further that whoever buys a commodity and sets out to bring the price but fails to do so within three days, the seller may demand the price, or he may sell or imprison the buyer, and the sale is annulled, leaving the buyer with no claim over it after the three days have elapsed. He said that whoever buys something with the option for a period, then spends on it and improves it, and then chooses to return it, must return what he received for the improvement, but he is entitled to recover what he spent on it. He also said that if the buyer purchases a commodity granting the option to another, then the option belongs to the one for whom it was designated.

Mentioning the rulings on defects:

The saying of the Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, has already been mentioned in explanation of the prohibition of fraud: whoever knows of a defect in what he is selling and conceals it is guilty of fraud. Likewise, nothing of what the seller knows should be withheld from the buyer, as the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's prayers be upon him and his family, said that sincere counsel is part of religion. He also said that it is not permissible for a Muslim to sell to his brother something in which he knows there is a defect without disclosing it, and it is not permissible for one who knows of such a defect to conceal it. Whoever disowns responsibility for a defect on the part of the seller must disclose it to the buyer along with his disavowal, and it is not sufficient for him to say "I sell to you with full innocence" without

<!-- page 29 -->
naming the specific defect he disavows. The buyer must see and acknowledge it; only then, if he does not see it and accepts the sale, does the seller's disavowal stand. He said, peace be upon him, that if a group purchases a commodity and divides it among themselves, and one of them sustains loss due to a defect in his share, he may recover the diminution in value from the seller. If the commodity has not yet been divided, or if one person purchased it all and part of it is found defective, he may recover the defect's value, unless he prefers to return the entire commodity if he wishes — even if the commodity is one that is sold by weight, measure, or the like, such as cotton, books, silk, or food, and the defect is found in part of it, with the seller having acted upon it in kind. If the buyer finds a defect in the commodity, he said, the seller should tell him: either keep it with its defect, or return it — there is no third option. If the buyer wishes to keep it and if he wishes to return it, he may do so; but if he requests compensation for the value of the defect, he has no right to that unless the seller agrees. He said, peace be upon him, that whoever buys a commodity and introduces a new use into it, or causes a defect in it, and then discovers an old defect that the seller did not disclose, the buyer may return it in its altered condition and the seller must accept it, or the buyer may keep it with its defect. If the seller had originally disclosed the defect and the buyer's silence constituted acceptance, then the buyer has no claim for the diminution in value. This may only be established through testimony to the fraud; if there is no such evidence, the seller may swear that he did not know of the defect, or the buyer may redirect the oath to the seller, who then swears that he did not know of the defect before the purchase or at the time of sale. Once the seller swears and it is established that he did not know, then it is not permissible for anyone else to take an oath, and the buyer then has the right to return the item or to recover the value of the defect if they mutually agree, or it may be assessed and valued. If the buyer abuses the oath, or had seen the defect, or benefited from the commodity after seeing it, or offered it for sale, then the sale is binding and there is no claim. It was narrated from the Commander of the Believers, may Allah's prayers be upon him, and from Muhammad ibn Ali ibn al-Husayn and Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon them, regarding a man who purchases a slave girl and rides her and then discovers a defect in her, that he has no right to return her but has the right to recover the value of the defect from whoever sold her to him, unless he finds her to be pregnant, in which case he returns her and also receives back half of her value. They said, peace be upon them, that a slave may be returned to the buyer with a guarantee for one year covering the conditions of sound health: madness, leprosy, and horn-related afflictions. If any of these occur after that period, there is nothing between the buyer and the established practice.

<!-- page 30 -->
Likewise, the calamity of death falls upon the seller, and this applies especially to slaves, distinct from the rest of animals. It was narrated from Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him, that he said the guarantee for a slave covers the gravest diseases, and that the calamity of death extends for three days. Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said that if the seller declares innocence to the buyer from the guarantee of sound health and the option of three days for all animals at the time of the sale contract, and the buyer accepts this declaration of innocence, then the buyer has no recourse to any of those claims. People today write into the documents of slave purchases the declaration of innocence from the guarantee of sound health and the option of three days, and into the documents of other animals the declaration of innocence from the option of three days. Al-Mahdi, may Allah's prayers be upon him, in what has been transmitted from him, ordered this to be written down so that people would not revert to disputes and prolong their quarrels over it. Whoever accepts it is bound by it, and whoever objects to it shall not be compelled. If the seller is satisfied, he is not obligated to mention this declaration of innocence; but if he does mention it, it becomes binding as described above. Al-Sadiq Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: there is no guarantee in a sale made with a declaration of innocence, nor in a sale of inheritance, nor for the condition of sound health, nor for the option of three days.

Mentioning the sale of Murabaha:

Selling by Murabaha is permissible when stated as buying and profiting. However, the pure Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt disliked the practice of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, of selling Murabaha on the condition that profit is made a reward proportional to the price, because that resembles riba. For if one says, "I will sell you this commodity with a profit of one-tenth of its price" or any similar formula, the increase is not merely a profit but is rather an addition to the price, which makes it resemble riba. This is the form of sale practiced by the merchants of Iraq, who say, "I will sell you this commodity for dowzadeh or for yazda" — Persian words meaning "for every ten, twelve" or "for every ten, one." Abu Abdullah Ja'far ibn Muhammad, peace be upon him, said that his father received goods from Egypt, prepared food for the merchants, and invited them to his home to purchase. He ordered them to be fed, and they ate; then he ordered the goods to be presented. They said, "We will take it from you at dowzadeh," and he replied, "No, but I bought these goods for ten thousand dinars and I am selling them to you for twelve thousand." They accepted and he gave the goods to them. He said, peace be upon him, that if someone buys goods and incurs expenses on them — such as freight, containers, and the like — he may sell them by Murabaha and include what he spent in the price, meaning he may say the total cost was such and such, or he need not specify. However,

<!-- page 31 -->
he should not say that he bought it for a price that includes his additional expenditure, as that would be a lie and would bring him to the boundary of riba, unless he specifies the purchase price, the profit, and the expenditure separately, or combines them clearly, saying "This cost me so much in total." He should not say he bought it for more than he actually paid. He was asked about a man who bought a piece of merchandise, divided it into parts, and wished to sell each part by Murabaha for what it had cost him. He said: No, unless he explains to the buyer that he has combined them for himself as he did. A man who bought a commodity by direct inspection and then sold it by Murabaha to a buyer who had not seen the original inspection, and the buyer later learned of this, may stand by the sale; it was said this is his right, or he may say to him at the time of the contract, "On this day I acquired it for such a price and I am selling it to you for such a price." He said: If someone buys a garment for one dinar in cash and pays it in dirhams, or pays a dinar in cash, he may sell it by Murabaha based on what he bought it for, without mentioning what he paid in currency. This is in accordance with what has been said, may Allah's prayers be upon him, that it is permissible to make gold into silver and silver into gold, and this applies when he bought it with what he bought of gold or silver and the price between him and the seller has been settled. He must include in the price what he spent on it, which is permissible to mention. The purchase is binding on the one who made the sale. He said, peace be upon him, that it is permissible for someone who buys his own slave and then sells him by Murabaha, because that does not diminish the value and is not something that must be compensated. Similarly, if he bought a beast and rode it, or a slave and used him, or a garment and wore it — unless there is resulting wear — he must inform the buyer of this, which is the safe course for the seller. If he does not inform the buyer, there is no liability upon him.

The narrations also arrived on this matter.

Mention of al-salam:

Allah said: "O you who believe, when you contract a debt for a specified term, write it down." (11) The pure Imams from the family of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, narrated that he said: Whoever enters into a sale for an unspecified term, or for something unspecified, has not truly made a sale. They said, peace be upon them, that al-salam has four conditions, and if any one of them is absent or violated, the contract is not valid. The first is the description of al-salam, which is to describe

(11) Al-Baqarah: 282


<!-- page 32 -->
A brief compendium of transmitted reports — Volume Two

The item being delivered must be described in terms of its measure, whether by volume or by weight according to its nature, and the place of delivery must be specified along with the time at which delivery is due. The pure Imams said that a salam contract is not discharged except by payment of the price before the two contracting parties part from the place where the contract was made. It has been indicated that if the price is not paid at the session, the transaction becomes a debt exchanged for a debt, and the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family) forbade that.

Ibn Muhammad (may Allah's blessings be upon him) held that whoever enters into a salam contract for the produce of a specific village or a specific locality is not permitted to do so unless it may reasonably be expected to be available; if no locality is specified, then the salam contract is permissible. The obligation falls upon the party receiving the advance payment, and the Imam (may Allah's blessings be upon him) permitted a salam contract with one who does not yet possess what he has committed to deliver, provided the item falls within his means when the time comes. At that point he acquires it, pays for it, and delivers it. The Imams also permitted salam contracts involving animals when the animal is known and precisely described.

When asked about a case in which a man enters a salam contract for a specific item and subsequently receives either less than that item or something in addition to it, the Imam said: if both parties consent, there is no harm in it. The Imams permitted salam contracts for well-known, described garments, and held that if a man takes back his capital or a portion of it without adding anything, that is permissible; but if he adds something, it is not permitted.

Ja'far ibn Muhammad (peace be upon him) was asked about a case in which the holder of the salam obligation pays the price to the one entitled to it, who then says, "Buy it for yourself." The Imam said that this fulfils the obligation in the manner of Malik's view. He also said that the holder should give the goods to someone other than himself and should not take the purchase for his own benefit.

He was asked about one who holds a salam obligation but cannot find the specific item at the time it falls due, yet finds others of the same kind — such as animals or offerings — so he pays their equivalent value, and this is acceptable. Likewise, if a man sold food in exchange for dirhams and when the due date arrived said, "I have no dirhams, but accept this food instead," there is no harm in the creditor taking food from him. If the debtor does have dirhams, the creditor may take whichever he wishes. The Imams regarded it as reprehensible to enter salam contracts for items that cannot be stored, such as fresh fruit, meat, and the like.

**On the conditions and rulings governing sale contracts:**

Al-Sadiq Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's blessings be upon him) said: Whoever purchases a property, and there is money with it, the money belongs to the seller unless the buyer stipulates otherwise in the contract; if the buyer does stipulate it, the seller has no claim to it. This applies similarly where the amount exceeds the price of a slave, for whatever a slave possesses and holds in his hands — if he is being sold — is part of his price. This does not fall within the category of riba that is prohibited, particularly when the transaction involves an offer, or when

<!-- page 33 -->
A brief compendium of transmitted reports — Volume Two

the item is a tangible object and the slave was purchased with one of the offers, or was himself an object, and the slave was purchased with a document, or was himself a document and the slave was purchased with a tangible object. In all such cases the sale contract is valid, there being no disagreement on this point.

The manner of contracting a sale is as follows: one man says to another, "I give you this servant of yours, or this commodity of yours, for such-and-such" — specifying something lawful to sell — and the other says, "I have sold it to you for that." Alternatively, the seller begins by saying, "I sell you this for such-and-such," and the buyer says, "I have bought it" or "I accept this purchase." The sale is thereby concluded between them, and they retain the right of option until they part from the place of contract — that is, until they physically separate. Either party who wishes to withdraw before they have parted may do so and the sale is annulled. Annulment can only occur through separation, as described.

Ja'far ibn Muhammad (peace be upon him) said regarding the case in which the buyer claims to have paid the price while it remains in his hand and the seller denies having received anything: if the transaction is of a kind that is commonly witnessed, the buyer's claim of payment is not accepted without evidence, and the seller must swear that he did not receive the price and has in no way obtained it from him. If the transaction is not of a kind that is commonly witnessed, then the statement of the claimant prevails.

He said: Whoever sells a sale and then claims he made an error in it — that he did not know its price at the time of sale or intended something else — if the discrepancy between the price he sold it for and its actual value falls within the range that commonly occurs in transactions of that kind, the sale stands and his claim is disregarded. If however the discrepancy is substantial, beyond what is ordinarily tolerated, the sale is returned to him. The buyer may be asked to swear that the seller did indeed make an error as he claims.

From Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali (may Allah's blessings be upon him): he was asked about a man who entrusted an agent to sell his goods, and the agent claimed to have sold them but the owner sought to reverse the sale and reclaim the commodity. The Imam said: one should examine the matter. If the price at which it was sold on the day of sale corresponds to its value, or exceeds it, in the manner customary among merchants, the sale is permissible and cannot be reversed merely because the value is higher. However, if the discrepancy is beyond what people ordinarily accept, the sale is returned. If the buyer wishes to bind the owner of the commodity by oath — that he did not authorise his agent to sell and did not ratify it after learning of this wrong — he has the right to demand that oath. If the owner declines to swear, the buyer has no further recourse.

Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali (may Allah's blessings be upon him) was asked about two agents jointly appointed on condition that they sell a particular item: if one of them sells it without the other, the sale is not valid unless it was made clear from the outset that either one of them

<!-- page 34 -->
A brief compendium of transmitted reports — Volume Two

is independently authorised when they act together. Abu 'Abd Allah Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's blessings be upon him) was asked about a man who was negotiating with his slave girl, and her owner sold her to him saying, "I have sold her to you for whatever price you wish." The buyer accepted the sale and then offered a price that the seller was not satisfied with. The Imam said: one should look at her value on the day of sale. If the price offered is less than her value, the seller is owed the difference, for even in a binding ruling it is not permissible to judge him unjustly.

He said: Whatever a judge or a ruler sells in discharge of an obligatory debt, or on behalf of a minor, or in the case of one ordered to sell — the latter bears no liability of trust or guarantee, for the proceeds belong to the one on whose behalf he was ordered to sell, whatever his liabilities may be, even if he has no money. As for a guardian or trustee or any similar party, if he generates profit it goes to the orphan, and if he incurs a loss it falls upon the one who traded in this manner, and he must make it good from the orphan's funds because he transgressed.

He said (peace be upon him) regarding a slave who was given money by his master, with which he purchased his own freedom and was emancipated: when the master learned of it, the Imam said that whatever money the slave had taken — and the slave remains in the master's ownership according to what the case was — if the money had been earned by the slave and had come to him without his master's permission, but the master had subsequently granted him that or given him something or provided it from a legitimate source, then the purchase of his own freedom is permissible. A slave may draw up his own written contract and may have it recorded by a scribe from his earnings.

It was narrated from the Imams (may Allah's blessings be upon them) that one who dies and has no heir other than a slave he owns: the slave is purchased from the estate — that is, if the master agrees to sell him — and the remainder of the estate is distributed as inheritance. If the master does not agree to sell, the slave does not inherit, and the inheritance belongs to the public treasury if there is no heir other than him.

He said: Selling a house for a house, a share for a share, a quarter for a quarter, and selling a portion of anything — all of this is permissible if both parties are aware of what they are transacting. From the Commander of the Faithful (may Allah's blessings be upon him): he adjudicated a case involving two men who had purchased a commodity from a man. After completing the sale with him they departed, and when they returned to pay the price, one of them told the other to collect the commodity from the seller. When the second man came, he paid half the price to the one who had collected the goods, making him a partner in it.

It was also narrated that certain people dealt with a woman of the Quraysh who managed a large sum of money. Two men came to her with money and deposited it all with her, saying, "Do not release it to either one of us without the other." Then one of them gave her a sign that he and the other had agreed upon between them, told her about it, and said, "He has sent me to collect the money, and this is the sign between him and you," so she released the money to him.

<!-- page 35 -->
A brief compendium of transmitted reports — Volume Two

He took it and left. Then the other man came to her and said, "The money," and she replied, "Your companion has taken it and conveyed your message and your agreed-upon sign between me and you." He said, "I sent nothing to you and gave him no such instruction." The matter was brought before 'Umar in his time, and he ruled against her for the money. Then the prayers of Allah came upon 'Ali (peace be upon him) to assist her, and he said to 'Umar: "Did they not tell her not to release the money to either one of them without the other?" He said yes. 'Ali said: "He has no right to hold her liable until he brings his companion with him." The woman was thus released and the trick perpetrated against her was exposed.

From Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's blessings be upon him): he said that an emissary of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family) came from Yemen, and when they reached al-Jahfa a slave girl from the captives was sold — a girl who had her mother with her. They brought the mother to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family) and he disapproved and ordered her to be returned, saying: "Do not separate kindred by sale."

Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's blessings be upon him) said: A man who purchases a commodity from another and then seeks to return it, but the seller refuses to accept the return, so the buyer remits part of the price in exchange for the seller accepting the return — the Imam said: let him take from the seller whatever the seller remitted, since the seller has dismissed him.
