But if the seller were to purchase another commodity from the buyer — separate from what the seller sold him — that is permissible, and dismissal is not effected by reducing the price.

**On debts:**

The pure Imams from the household of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) narrated that he said: Allah the Almighty is with the debtor until he fulfils his debt, provided he intends to do so and the debt is not for something that Allah hates. He also said: The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family) — one who releases a captive or frees a beloved one and is found insolvent — will be placed under the shade of the Throne on a day when there is no shade but His.

He said: "Whoever lends a loan, for him is the reward of charity." Then he said: "Whoever lends a loan, for him is the reward of charity every day." Someone asked: "O Messenger of Allah, you said before that whoever lends a loan has the reward of charity, and now you say every day?" He replied: "If he lends a loan and then time passes, for every day that elapses he receives the reward of charity — until he collects it."

'Ali (peace be upon him) stood to ask about a debt he owed but found he had nothing to give at the time. Someone said to him: "Shall I tell you something better than that?" He said: "Yes, what is it?" The man said: "I heard the Messenger of Allah (peace be upon him and his family) say: if you have patience with a debt, Allah the Almighty will decree its fulfilment for you."


<!-- page 36 -->
In this volume, we turn to matters of loans and their related rulings. It is said regarding patience that it is a mountain in Yemen. As for the prescribed supplication, one should say: "O Allah, suffice me with what You have made lawful, sparing me from what You have made unlawful, and enrich me through Your grace so that I have no need of any other than You." The Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him, also stated that none of you should ride a horse or carry belongings belonging to a man from whom he has taken a loan. Ja'far ibn Muhammad, upon whom be the prayers of Allah, likewise disapproved of a creditor descending upon his debtor, eating his food, drinking his drink, or feeding from his fodder. Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn Ali, upon whom be the prayers of Allah, said: every loan that yields a benefit is riba. This applies whether the benefit arises from a stipulation or an intention — as in the case of a man who descends upon his debtor and eats his food and feeds from his fodder — for perhaps he does so on account of the loan. However, if the benefit is freely given and exists as a customary practice between them with no connection to the loan, there is nothing wrong with it. It has also been related from them that whoever advances dirhams or dinars in exchange for better coin — there is nothing wrong with that, provided no such condition is stipulated.

'Abdullah Ja'far ibn Muhammad, upon whom be the prayers of Allah, was asked about a man whose client or associate comes to him, and he lends him money so that the borrower may benefit from it, buy and sell with it, while the broker lends to people against their crops until he sells on their behalf and they repay him — otherwise he would not lend to them at all. This was permitted if the arrangement was well-known, though disapproval was expressed for any loan from which the lender derives a direct benefit. The Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, forbade the advance of a benefit. It has been related from him, from al-Husayn ibn Ali, from Muhammad ibn Ali ibn al-Husayn, and from Ja'far ibn Muhammad, upon whom be the prayers of Allah, that they all regarded it as reprehensible for a man to borrow money with land as security — whether he gives that land to the lender or to another — and they did not consider this allowable merely because it was a loan.

It has also been related from them, upon whom be the prayers of Allah, that transactions conducted on credit are permitted, provided the contract is properly formed. Thus, if a man has a cleric who is lawfully engaged with him and says: "I cannot find what I need to spend, so sell it to me on a deferred basis and I will sell it and spend from the proceeds" — and he does so — there is nothing wrong with that. However, if the arrangement is merely verbal and not a genuine sale, it is not permissible. For example, if one says: "Lend me one hundred dinars for two hundred" — or any similar increase framed as a loan — yet then sells it in a valid transaction such that the value increases by that amount and the term is set, this is disapproved. The proper course is to leave aside mention of the increase in Islamic terms and to proceed with a sale contract concluded on

<!-- page 37 -->
sound terms after negotiation, which is permissible. This is analogous to a man and a woman who discuss an illicit relationship and then, having set it aside, enter into a valid marriage — that negotiation and coordination in the context of immorality is disapproved and forbidden, yet it does not invalidate the marriage they subsequently entered into, nor anything arising from it. Rather, when they return to lawful conduct with sincere repentance, all is well, God willing.

Abu 'Abdullah Ja'far ibn Muhammad, upon whom be the prayers of Allah, was asked about a man who says to another man: "Buy me such-and-such goods, and I will buy them from you after a given period at such-and-such an increase." He said: there is nothing wrong with this, provided the man buys after he has taken ownership and then grants the purchaser the debt on agreed terms. If the purchaser then needs the goods before the term is complete, it is permissible for them to agree to accelerate payment or for the purchaser to offer part of the debt in exchange for an extension on the remainder — provided no increase above the original capital is added.

**Mentioning Hawala and Sponsorship**

Hawala is when a man has a debt owed by another man and transfers that debt to a third party. Sponsorship is the act of bearing a burden on behalf of another — also called guaranty, surety, pledge of the person, and undertaking — and whoever bears such a burden is obliged to fulfill what he has undertaken. The Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him, said: "The leader is a guarantor," and the leader, as we have mentioned, is the one who bears the burden. As it is said: *"They said: We are missing the king's goblet, and whoever brings it shall have a camel's load"* (Yusuf: 71), and *"They said: By Allah, you know well that we have not come to make mischief in the land"* (Yusuf: 72) — that is, the bearer of the burden. Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn Ali and Abu 'Abdullah Ja'far ibn Muhammad, upon whom be the prayers of Allah, both said: I am a guarantor.

Ja'far ibn Muhammad, upon whom be the prayers of Allah, said: if a man refers a creditor to another party for the settlement of a debt, then the creditor has the right to pursue whichever of the two he wishes — either the original debtor or the one to whom he has been referred — unless the original debtor has been fully discharged. If the discharge was made at the time of the transfer, then the creditor may not pursue anyone except the one to whom he was referred, and he has no further claim against the original debtor who was discharged. If two men jointly guarantee a third party's debt, the creditor may take from whichever of the two guarantors he wishes, unless it was stipulated that the guarantee is divided equally between them. If no such stipulation was made and each gave an absolute guarantee, then he may take the full amount from whichever of them he is able to reach.

<!-- page 38 -->
If he so wishes, and if it was stipulated in the contract of guaranty that each of them is a guarantor on behalf of the other, then he may take from whichever he wishes, and the one from whom he takes may recover half of what was owed from his co-guarantor. If one guarantor is added after another, they are all equally liable as guarantors, and the creditor may take from whichever of them he wishes, unless one has been discharged. If the guarantor guarantees with money or property, he must fulfill that guarantee unless the creditor discharges him from it; the guarantor cannot bring another guarantor in his place. If the guarantee is a guarantee of the person, then the guarantor must produce that person to the one who holds the guaranty, in fulfillment of the agreed condition, and he disavows himself thereby — for nothing has been discharged from him. But if he bears the burden absolutely with respect to the person and is asked to produce him, he must do so when called upon. If the guarantee pertains to money, there is no recourse for the creditor against the guarantor until the appointed term has expired; if the guarantor fails to produce the person at that time, he is liable to pay. However, the guarantor's obligation is voided only by death: if the guaranteed person dies, the guarantor has no further obligation toward the creditor. If the guaranteed person is absent and the guarantor says, "I will pay what he owes" — yet what he owes is unknown — the guarantor is not bound beyond what is proven. If the guaranty concerns a matter that necessarily involves bringing the sponsored person before the one holding the guaranty — for an oath, compulsion, or the like — then the guarantor must produce him unless the person dies or the creditor discharges the guarantor. It has also been said: there is no guaranty in cases of prescribed legal punishment (hadd). Whoever is liable for a hadd — whether execution, amputation, or retribution — cannot be released on guaranty in that matter. Rather, the obligation is fulfilled by the testimony of witnesses; he is held in custody until the testimony is established, at which point he is detained and cannot be released on surety, because if he were released he might escape the prescribed penalty. Sponsorship does not bind the slave to act as guarantor, and a slave may not engage in business transactions unless his master has given him permission to do so.

**Mentioning Interdiction (al-Hajr) and the Safih**

Interdiction is an established legal concept. Allah the Almighty has said: *"And test the orphans"* (al-Nisa': 6), meaning try them, even when they reach adulthood — that is, puberty — and the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, has prohibited the wasting of wealth. Allah the Almighty has also said: *"And do not waste; indeed, the wasteful are the brothers of the devils."* A child is therefore under interdiction with respect to his wealth by virtue of

<!-- page 39 -->
the Book, until he reaches the age of discernment and sound judgment, and it is recognized from him that he understands and can manage his money — only then is his money returned to him. Interdiction also applies on account of foolishness and wastefulness: if these reach a sufficient degree and constitute genuine waste, the interdiction remains in force. If such behavior manifests after he has come of age, he is placed under interdiction. 'Ali ibn Abi Talib, upon whom be the prayers of Allah, imposed interdiction upon 'Abdullah ibn Ja'far ibn Abi Talib, upon whom be the prayers of Allah, when it was established that he had squandered his wealth, and brought the matter to 'Uthman during his caliphate, who ordered that he be placed under interdiction. 'Uthman accordingly imposed it. Then, after some time, 'Abdullah purchased a salt marsh for sixty thousand, and 'Uthman said: "By Allah, I am not pleased." He came and said: "Do not impose interdiction upon your nephew."

As for the insane person, interdiction is imposed upon him as well. Whoever is placed under interdiction shall have his expenses met from his own wealth in a fitting manner, and a guardian shall be appointed to protect his wealth and spend on him from it. If the foolish person and the wasteful person and the insane person are judged sound by those qualified to testify, they shall be placed under interdiction, and if they later demonstrate reform, the interdiction shall be lifted from them. Their prior dispositions of their wealth are void.

**Bankruptcy**

If a man is in debt and becomes insolvent — unable to find anything with which to repay, having nothing to sell and no funds to settle the debt — he is not to be imprisoned on account of his insolvency. If he owes debts to a group of creditors and has funds that do not suffice to cover all the debts, each creditor participates proportionally in those funds: each takes as much as corresponds to his debt, and the shortfall is reduced in the same proportion. For example, if a man goes bankrupt and owes one man one hundred dinars and another man two hundred dinars, and he has only thirty dinars in hand, then the owner of the hundred dinars takes ten and the owner of the two hundred takes twenty. The same calculation applies whether the debts increase or decrease. If some of what he holds is subsequently found or if some of it is sold, the proceeds are distributed among the creditors in proportion to the remaining debts until they are all satisfied. Even if he later acquires a surplus or profit beyond the repayment of his debts, that surplus belongs to all the creditors and is not retained by him alone. It is not permissible for a bankrupt person to sell what he holds without the creditors' oversight, until their money is paid. They are to investigate the sale of his assets and ensure the best price is obtained among them. If it is ascertained that no price increase is achievable, they agree and approve the sale, which is then conducted and its proceeds distributed. If the bankrupt person refuses to sell, or one of the creditors appeals to the sultan, and it is established by testimony that the sale has been properly investigated and that the matter of selling and distributing among the creditors has been determined — if the debts are thereby discharged, they are discharged; if some creditors are satisfied while others are not, the surplus is returned to him; and if some creditors are satisfied and others are not, the assets are sold to the extent of the outstanding debts.


<!-- page 40 -->

Volume Two
A Book That Is Brief on the Effects

The bankrupt's acknowledgment of debt is not accepted after insolvency is declared, unless the one acknowledged comes forward with proof, or with the sincere testimony of a trusted witness while he is solvent. If he acknowledges a debt at that point, it is permissible. If something passes through his hands, he is entitled to his share of it, but it does not extend to the rest of his creditors — he remains entitled to it until he receives what is owed to him.

Abu Abdullah Ja'far ibn Muhammad, peace be upon him, was asked about a man who buys a slave and frees him, or a concubine who bears him a child, or something given as charity, and then he goes bankrupt. He said: If he bought that at a fair price while he was solvent, and his bankruptcy had not yet been established, and he went bankrupt afterwards, then the manumission and the charity stand. The seller is treated like a creditor among his creditors for whatever remains in his hands. If the man was poor and had no resources, then neither the manumission nor the charity is permitted, since he had nothing to give. It has been narrated from the People of the House, peace be upon them, that the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, held that the mother of a child may be sold for the price of her own freedom — meaning that if he buys her, he owes no one else; but if he has the means, the seller takes his share along with the other creditors. What remains of the debt stays as an obligation upon the freed person, and he is asked for it whenever he is able, so he must give it. Whichever is easier — the freed man or the freed woman — may be asked for it; the easiest path is taken. But the freed man may not be compelled to return to service as a concubine or as one whom he freed, nor may he be asked for what remains in the same way as if he were still enslaved. If the one who freed them has nothing and there is a surplus in their value beyond what was owed, preference is given to freeing them at their assessed value. If it involves a charitable gift, preference is given to the charity going to the recipient and the remainder being returned. Whoever has debts and has slaves whose sale would cover those debts — if he frees them — it is not permitted, and they are sold to pay the debt, provided he has no other funds. If he does have other funds, what was in his hands and what he spent, and what some creditors received and others did not, all stand: what he spent and what he sold in that regard is permissible for the one who received it. It is not permissible for a person to pay off a debt by selling or buying unless it is done lawfully. It is forbidden if the deceased becomes bankrupt and the wife is deprived of her rights. If the debtor or the opponent of the debt rises, the one closest in right is the one who has established his claim or had it established against him. When a judgment is issued and the man claims he has nothing, and the one relying on it is contradicted, whether it concerns a debt or something that reached him — he must prove that he lacked it and that it left his hands. Even if what was required of him did not reach him in the form of something obligatory, such as a felony liability, bail, hawala, the maintenance of a dependent, or a dowry,

<!-- page 41 -->

or something similar — then the declaration of insolvency is affirmed by his oath, and the right to contest it belongs to the one with proof. As for what he claims to own, if he proves any of it he is held to it; if he cannot prove it, it remains a debt. He is asked for it when it becomes easier to collect, and when it appears that he has something in his hands, his property is sold and his debts are paid from the proceeds.

Even if those around him testify that he is wealthy and that the money his debt requires is absent from his hands, he is imprisoned and beaten until he spends it. If he says to his opponent when confronted, "I will settle your right and give you your debt, but it is like this," without claiming bankruptcy, that is taken at face value and he is imprisoned — even if the opponent is the more powerful and has apparent assets — until the judge imprisons him and compels him to sell and beats him if he delays in selling. If he acknowledges or it is proven against him that he has funds and he refrains from paying, he is imprisoned at the request of his opponent and beaten until he spends what he has.

Mention of Tenant Farming and Musaqah

Tenant farming involves a partnership in cultivation on the condition that each party has a defined share of the produce, or that a man sows on another man's land using his own cattle or seed on the condition that he gives him something from the yield.

Abu Abdullah Ja'far ibn Muhammad, peace be upon him, was asked about this, and he permitted it. He said that the Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, gave the land of Khaybar to the Jews on the condition that they would cultivate it and receive half of what it produced, and he divided it with them on that basis. The land had palms and trees, and those who worked it received a third or a quarter. Whatever the two parties agreed upon, whether less or more, is permissible. It is also permissible to rent land for a fixed period to cultivate it for a known and understood consideration. It is permissible to rent land for wheat on the condition that wheat is grown in it. The prayers of Allah be upon him — it is permissible for the tenant farmer to do what the parties stipulated regarding labor, plowing, cattle, and so forth, and according to what they agreed upon, whether the land belongs to both of them or to one of them. He disapproved of stipulating a known share for the full moon and a separate share for the land and a separate share for the cattle; rather, each of them should stipulate a known share for himself from the total yield.

Musaqah is work on land that contains trees, carried out by a worker on the condition that he receives a defined share of what is produced. Ja'far ibn Muhammad, peace be upon him, said: if a man is given a piece of land to plow, water, build up, and it contains pomegranates, palms, fruits, legumes, or the like, and the condition is that he receives half of what is produced, or whatever they agree upon, then it is permissible. If he gives it to him

<!-- page 42 -->

to build his land and cultivate it, and he planted it but then claimed that the landowner disapproved and the landowner denied it — the saying in that case is with the landowner, affirmed by his oath, unless it was known when the planting was done that the sower had permission. In that case, the sower swears and he is entitled to the equivalent of a tenant's wage for that land, and the same applies if the lord of the land had no knowledge of it and has no proof against it. He then swore that he had not given him much of what was claimed, and the farmers are fined like the tillers of the land. The owner of the land has no right to uproot the sowing from it. If the farmers plowed it and transgressed, and it is proven against them, and neither the plaintiff nor the defendant swears after the oath was referred, and it is not proven that the landowner was transgressing — then the owner of the land is free either to take the equivalent in compensation or to pay the sower's expenses and cultivation costs and take the seedlings, and he has no right to uproot them, because uprooting the sowing causes corruption. He said: if a man plowed land and another man asked to share with him what he had plowed on the condition that he pay half the seed and half the maintenance costs, and they agreed on that, it is permissible. He said: there is no harm in giving a man a ruined piece of land, and whoever builds it for a fixed period on the condition that all that is produced during that period is his — when the agreed period has elapsed, the land is returned to its owner. Likewise, if he has a water channel on it and a man accepts it with its produce, there is nothing wrong with that. He may sow it, and if he leaves it without the landowner preventing him, there is no claim between them. He may do with it as he wishes for the period given — if he wishes to plow it and if he wishes to leave it — unless it was stipulated that he has no right to leave it unplowed. The authorization is to plow it and to hold to that; it is a condition placed on him and his contract binds him to it.

He was asked about the people of a village who had a tribute obligation, and the Sultan pressed them, so they assigned it to a man on the condition that what he took would suffice for them — whether the same or more — and that what remained after what was taken would go to them as a surplus. He said: if they agreed to that, it is permissible, and the one who undertook it from them is entitled to the surplus they agreed upon.

Mention of Rents

The Imams from the household of the Messenger of Allah, peace be upon them all, narrated that there was found in a sheathed sword a book in which was written the curse of Allah, the angels, the prophets, and the messengers — and the curse of the Prophet Muhammad, peace be upon him — upon whoever claims as his father one who is not his father, or claims as his patron one who is not his patron, or oppresses a hired laborer his wage, or encroaches upon landmark boundaries.

<!-- page 43 -->

Volume Two
A Book That Is Brief on the Effects

Ali, the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, said: I found this book in the sheath of the sword of the Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, and I know it. This is a lengthy matter not suited to be mentioned here; it is mentioned in the Book of the Imamate. And Allah the Almighty said: "Say: I do not ask you for any reward for it except affection in kinship" (17). In this too there is a long discourse, which belongs in the Book of the Imamate.

The consensus of what was narrated from the Imams, peace be upon them, is that leasing oneself and those under one's care, and whatever one possesses — whether a craft, a ship, land, a dwelling, or anything else — is permissible in all its forms. The reward for that is lawful and permitted, and it is permissible to earn it. A lease in this case is valid if the parties contract it for a fixed period and a well-understood matter. It is permissible, and it is not permissible to do any of that if it involves something that is unlawful to earn or to do.

This is a foundational principle from which many branches derive, and rulings flow from it accordingly. The one who undertakes it is not permitted to lease himself, nor to lease what she does not own and to which he has no right. It is not permissible for one who owns what is not lawfully his to own to rent it — as with someone who has seized a free person, or who takes certain amusements or things that are not lawful for him; he does not deserve its value and it is not permissible for anyone else to receive it. It is not permissible for him to lease what he does not own, and it is not permissible to lease what is owned by someone who uses it for unlawful purposes — such as a man who rents himself, his cattle, his tools, or his ship for work involving intoxicants, or for carrying them, or for anything similarly impermissible.

Ja'far ibn Muhammad, peace be upon him, was asked about taking a wage for teaching the Quran. He considered it and said: A man said to the Commander of the Faithful, Ali, peace be upon him, "I love you, O Commander of the Faithful." He replied, "But I am displeased with you." The man asked why, and he said: "Because you take a wage for teaching the Quran, and you have heard the Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, say that whoever took the teaching of the Quran as a means of livelihood would be degraded on the Day of Resurrection." Permission was given, however, to take wages for teaching writing, literature, and other things that teachers teach boys, and he was given the reward for it. If it was something unlawful or disapproved, they did not give it to him; rather, they disapproved of this wage for teaching the Quran specifically. If the teacher teaches the boys the Quran along with other things,

(17) Al-Shura 23


<!-- page 44 -->
If it is permissible to take a wage for a given service, there is nothing wrong with doing so — the wage is lawful for what is permissible and not lawful for what is not. It is narrated that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) married a woman to one of his companions on the condition that he teach her a surah of the Qur'an. The hadith on that matter is complete in itself and indicates that such an arrangement may render the marriage valid even without a named dowry, as we will mention in the section on marriage.

Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali (peace be upon him) was asked about a man who recites the Qur'an while another man prepares food as people customarily do, then invites those who recited with him — including the one who was reciting to him. He said: "There is nothing wrong with that, unless it is done specifically for the sake of the Qur'an."

It is related from them (peace be upon them) that taking a wage for ruqyah using the Book of Allah and His names is permissible. It is narrated that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family) dispatched a company of companions who passed through one of the Arab encampments. They stopped there, and a leader of that encampment was stung. The people of the encampment came to the companions and asked whether any of them could perform ruqyah. Some of them recited the opening chapter of the Book over him, and he recovered. They were given thirty sheep, which they accepted. When they returned to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family) and informed him of what had occurred, he permitted them to keep what they had taken.

Abu 'Abd Allah al-Sadiq Ja'far ibn Muhammad (prayers of Allah be upon him) was asked about a man who gives another man a wage on the condition that he teach him a trade and work alongside him. He said that this falls within what has been mentioned: if the trade is something permissible, it is permitted. Whoever hires or is hired to teach something impermissible — such as singing, entertainment, and the like — it is not permissible.

On the matter of the muezzin: they held that the Commander of the Faithful (peace be upon him) said that whoever sits as a muezzin and takes nothing for it will have a reward. However, he said it is permissible for the muezzin to receive a stipend from the public treasury. He disliked, however, the muezzin taking a wage directly from those for whom he calls the adhan. Similarly, he disliked an imam taking a wage from those he leads in prayer, or a judge from those between whom he adjudicates. They are permitted, however, to receive stipends from the House of Wealth (Bayt al-Mal).

Abu 'Abd Allah Ja'far ibn Muhammad (prayers of Allah be upon him) was asked about the broker's wage — whether he should be hired by the seller or the buyer. He said it is permissible, provided that the buying and selling from which he earns his hire is itself permissible. He was also asked about a broker who is given a commodity to sell and finds a price for it, provided he does not exceed that. They said to them: peace be upon the hired worker who spoils or destroys what he has been hired to do — such as a cutter who cuts a garment, or a tailor or dyer who ruins it,

<!-- page 45 -->
or who cuts it wrongly, or deviates from what he was instructed, or a porter or carrier who breaks what he was paid to transport. If the goods are surrendered to him and he takes custody of them, then he is responsible if he is the one who caused them to be broken or damaged while in his possession. However, there is nothing against the owner of the animal if the hired man or porter claims that what he was carrying was wrenched from his hands and the owner denies it. The proof is upon the claimant, and the oath is upon the one who denies that he knew it had gone from him. The hired worker or porter would be held liable only if there were evidence against him. If the evidence shows that he stole from his own hands, then there is nothing upon him.

It is permitted — peace be upon them — to lease and hire by the eye with offers. Ja'far ibn Muhammad (prayers of Allah be upon him) was asked about this and said: "There is nothing wrong with that." He was asked about a man who rents by the quarter, and it was said: if he stayed for a day, should he be obligated to pay for a month or a year? He said that he should proceed accordingly.

He was asked about two parties who dispute the payment of the rental (kara'). He said: the owner of the house may take a prepayment, but the renter says, "I will not pay until the end of the month or year." He said: if they stipulated this as a condition, then it stands as they contracted. If it was not stipulated, then the rent may be taken day by day.

He was asked about a man who rents a house that has trees in it and stipulates the fruit as part of the rental contract. He said: if the fruit was blossoming at the time of the contract, or some of it was present, then that is permissible; otherwise it is not permissible. On the voiding of the rental contract when they conclude it jointly, and he was asked about a man who owns a house that was partially demolished by inheritance, or whose rooms were damaged by rain, and the renter repaired it at the owner's expense — he said the owner cannot be compelled to do so, but if the damage harms the renter, then the renter may reduce the rent and vacate, retaining the right to what he already occupied. If the damage does not constitute harm, the rental contract is not voided.

He said: even if the renter does not stipulate that the owner must repair what was inherited or clean its facilities, that is still incumbent upon the owner, even without stipulation, because it is among the things that are known to be obligatory upon the owner of the house. He said (peace be upon him) that it is not permissible for the renter to introduce anything into the house that would harm it, unless he stipulates that in the rental contract.

He was asked about a dispute between a renter and owner over the rent — the owner claiming more and the renter claiming less — whether the renter has occupied the house or not. He said: they should both take an oath, and whichever of them tires of the oath, the saying of the other prevails. If they swear, or one refuses the oath, it follows accordingly. He was asked (peace be upon him):

<!-- page 46 -->
He said: it is permissible to draw up a shared deed just as it is permissible to sell it. A man lives in another man's house and claims he lived in it for a lesser rent, while the owner of the house claims it was more. There is no dispute between them, and the owner of the house swears that he did not rent it to him for less. The resident is then liable for the value of the kara' unless that value exceeds what the owner claimed — in that case the owner takes only what he acknowledged as the rent and nothing more.

If the resident claims that the owner of the house ordered him to spend on it and make repairs, and he did so, but the owner denies it, then between them the owner of the house swears that he did not order him to do so, and the resident has the right to a rebuttal.

He was asked about a man who rents from another man a house in which goods have been stored, and some of the parties agree to have him removed, so they contract with him, yet he is reluctant to remove the goods. He said: he has no obligation to remove them except to the extent needed for the occupant of the house to dwell therein.

He was asked about a house that is damaged, or part of it, by someone who did something not stipulated in the rental contract. He said: he guarantees what was damaged because of that, unless it is something that people customarily do and it was not stipulated — in that case there is no guarantee if it falls within what is customarily understood, not whenever the renter does it must he stipulate it.

He was asked about an animal or a ship that is damaged before reaching the destination to which it was hired. He said: whoever hired the animal or the ship and inspected it and took custody of it, the rental is voided and he owes only the proportional rent for the distance already traveled. If he hired a carrier and did not specify a particular animal or ship, then the carrier must replace what was damaged with something equivalent to carry the load to the stipulated destination, and the full rental is due.

He was asked about one who rents an animal for a known number of days to ride it, load it, grind with it, or tread with it, mentioning only what he intends to use it for. He said: it is permissible, and he may use it appropriately. He must not exceed proper use, and the owner of the animal cannot cut short the period. If he does not specify what he intends to do with it, and then wishes to put it to a use that harms or diminishes it, he does not have that right.

He was asked about one who hires an animal or a ship from a man to carry a known load on the condition that he arrive at a certain time, and he arrives within that time and delivers it, and the carrier is rewarded accordingly and owes a similar reward. It was narrated from 'Ali (prayers of Allah be upon him) that he decreed that a man who hired an animal to a named destination and exceeded it

<!-- page 47 -->
— so he carried it beyond that — did not make the excess a forfeiture. Al-Sadiq Ja'far ibn Muhammad (prayers of Allah be upon him) said: if the animal did not perish and the renter exceeded with it the stipulated limit, the owner of the property has the right to take from him a reward commensurate with the excess if he wishes, or to take compensation for the diminishment caused by that excess — whichever he wishes — but he may not take both together. Similarly, if he takes it for a day or a few days beyond the period and then confines it, the owner has the choice to take either the proportional rent for what elapsed after the expiry of the condition, or the equivalent hire rate, even if it perishes. Confining it after the stipulated period makes him a guarantor of its value.

He was asked about a dispute between two parties over the distance — one claiming a far destination and the owner of the animal saying it is close. They agree on the dispute, and the proof is upon the one who claims the greater, while the oath is upon the carrier (Makari). If they agree on the destination but differ on the route, the proof falls upon the one claiming the more difficult of the two routes, and the right is with the other. If the two routes are equal and they differed at the time of the contract as to which of them applied, and he neither rode nor proceeded, they both take an oath between them. Whoever violates the oath, the right of the owner prevails against him. If he did ride, he was asked about the man who hires transport to a country — the Makari reaches the first settlement of that country and wants the renter to disembark there, while the renter insists on reaching the far end, and they did not name a specific place in the rental contract within that country. This is like a man who travels to Iraq, or to Khurasan, or to the Levant, or to Africa, or to similar lands. He said: the reference in such a matter is to the main city that is the principal of that region — the one intended by it, the one famous for it, and the one known to the people.

He was asked about land afflicted by a calamity such as flooding, drought, or the drying up of a spring or river by which it was irrigated. He said: if the renter wishes to spend on it from his own resources for the year, then the owner of the land is obligated to spend accordingly; otherwise the renter may occupy it for as long as he benefits from it.

He was asked about a rental contract in which an option is stipulated. He said: what is conditioned upon is as follows — what is permissible remains permissible, and what is not permissible remains not permissible, and the rental contract remains in its state.

He was asked about a man who delivers grain to a miller or sesame to

<!-- page 48 -->


<!-- page 48 -->
A concise book on the narrated reports, Volume Two

It is stipulated that he should give the worker a reward for what he makes, and he said, "This is not permissible." The timing must be specified for that, and the miller and the oil-presser are entitled to the reward for their work. The owner of the grain or sesame takes back whatever each of them produces. He was asked about the earnings from cupping, and he saw nothing wrong with it. He said, "The Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him, and the Imams, peace be upon them, gave it to them." They continue to give it to this day; yet even if what they gave was from something unlawful, it is still permissible to accept it. He was asked about the craftsman whom a customer approaches wanting work done, who describes to him something he cannot find — such as a tailor who is shown a garment and says he will make one like it and cut it accordingly, yet cannot find the material to cover it — or the goldsmith who says he can do such-and-such work for a known amount of gold, but after beginning to cast the gold and silver finds he is unable to complete it; or one who presents gold or silver and declares it of good quality, yet it proves to be poor. He said, peace be upon him, that each of them speaks according to the extent of his knowledge and the best of his judgment, and if he errs there is nothing against him — unless it is established that he knew of the defect, or it becomes clear that he intended to deceive the owner with what he had corrupted.

**On Pledges:**

"O you who believe, if you contract a debt for a specified term, write it down." (Al-Baqarah: 282.) This indicates that a pledge is only valid when it fulfills the conditions of a pledge. Whenever it is permissible to sell something, it is permissible to pledge it. Whoever pledges something in the customary manner is entitled to receive from it what the common person would receive in a sale. It is not permissible for the mortgagee to derive benefit from the pledge. If the pledged item perishes, it is from the pledgor's wealth, unless the pledgee caused its destruction. If the pledge is destroyed, the pledgee has no right to demand another item in its place; he has nothing but the right to demand his debt once the pledge is dissolved. If the pledgor and the pledgee disagree, the burden of proof lies with the one claiming the favor — that is, the pledgee — while the pledgor has the right of oath. If they then disagree over the pledge's value after its destruction, the burden of proof is likewise on the one claiming the credit for it, as he is the current possessor, and the pledgee swears to what he claims of the credit in it. The mortgagee may not sell the pledge upon maturity except by order of a judge, after the debt has been established before him.

(18) Al-Baqarah: 282
(19) Al-Baqarah: 283

<!-- page 49 -->
A concise book on the narrated reports, Volume Two

If the pledgor is absent, the judge orders that it be sold on his behalf and his right paid from the proceeds, just as the property of an absent debtor is sold. The judge acts on the pledgor's behalf in the matter. If the mortgagee sells the pledge — whether at the time of maturity, after it, or before it — without the pledgor's inclusion in that transaction, and he derives benefit from the pledge or exploits it, this is not permissible. The pledged item may not be sold, given away, donated, or freed if it is a slave, nor may it be taken out of the pledgor's possession in any way until the pledge is redeemed. The mortgagee may not sell it. If he is a merchant from whom the pledgor borrowed, the ruling on his debt — for which the item was pledged — applies at the time of maturity. The pledge is the one who buys it and settles its debt. If he wishes it to be sold to him at the time of its due date, he cannot appoint himself as agent to sell it on his own behalf; he must submit the matter to the judge, who will arrange the sale for the absent party. If the debt is proven before the judge, he will sell it on the pledgor's behalf and settle the debt, acting without the pledge itself. Even if the pledgor and all parties with an interest in the pledge agree to sell the pledged item at the time of maturity and proceed to sell it, bearing witness to that and striving to maximize its price and conducting the sale properly — there is nothing wrong with this, even if the pledgor accuses the mortgagee of wrongdoing. And if the mortgagee wishes to purchase the pledge himself, it is permissible for him to do so if they mutually agree, and the debt is offset against the purchase price between them, with the credit settled accordingly.

**On Partnership:**

Partnership in wealth, according to the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt, may Allah's prayers be upon them, is permissible when two men or a group contract it and each of those forming the partnership contributes money from his own wealth and mingles it with the money of the partnership contract until it becomes a single undifferentiated sum used for buying and selling. Whatever profit arises is shared between them and whatever loss is incurred is borne by them in proportion to what they have each contributed. Neither of them has the right to sell, buy, or initiate any action in what they have jointly contributed except with the knowledge and at the command of the other. Each of them must also allow his partner to do what he sees as permissible for him to do. He may not accuse his partner of betrayal unless he says to him, "You have betrayed me," or "You wished to buy and sell for yourself," or "What I have done I intended to consume the money," or something similar — in which case his partner may swear to him that he did not do that. If the money is held jointly between the two partners, it is stipulated that the profit and the loss

<!-- page 50 -->
Volume Two — A concise book on the narrated reports

are both according to their agreed terms. If they stipulate a different arrangement, then the profit follows what they stipulated, while the loss is proportional to the capital. If their capital contributions differ such that one of them contributes more than the other, the profit is likewise according to what they have agreed upon, and the loss is in proportion to the capital. If the money belongs to only one of them, this constitutes mudaraba — speculation — and the one who has no money is the mudarib, the working partner. They stipulate that he shall be the one who works with the money and trades with it, or they agree together on whatever they have stipulated. The profit between them is based on what they have contracted, and the loss falls on the owner of the money alone and not on the mudarib. If the owner of the money guarantees the mudarib against any shortfall or loss he incurs, this guarantee renders the arrangement no longer a valid mudaraba or partnership; it becomes instead a loan. The borrower retains whatever gain he earns from it, and he must guarantee it as he would for himself. If the mudarib violates what has been stipulated by the owner of the money — in what he buys, what he trades in, where he travels, or other conditions stipulated in the mudaraba contract — then the profit between them is still according to what they agreed, but if a loss occurs or the money passes out of his hands, he is its guarantor, having transgressed the terms. If a man gives money to another man to work with it, trade in it, and earn a specified profit from it as a fixed amount, this is not permissible and is not binding upon the one to whom it was given; it is riba, which is not permissible for him. He must return whatever he took and repent to Allah Almighty from that — unless the one who paid it to him renounces the profit, for then what was paid is halal. There is no riba between a master and his slave, because the slave's wealth belongs to his master. If one of the partners dies, the partnership is dissolved, and any partner may annul it and dissolve it whenever he wishes. If the partner or the mudarib is accused, he swears an oath. The partnership is disliked when it involves offers made in a manner that distinguishes and does not mix the contributions. If the two partners participate in what is held separately by each, it belongs to its owner, whether it increases or decreases. If they agree to a partnership between them, each of them sells to the other half of the separate goods in his possession at a known price, they commit to that, and they thereby subscribe to a valid partnership. It is disliked for a Muslim to enter into partnership with a dhimmi and for a believer to partner with one who disagrees with him in religion, since such a partner may engage in buying and selling that the believer does not permit and cannot avoid, because that introduces what is unlawful and riba-tainted into his wealth. If the believer or Muslim oversees the buying and selling without the dhimmi or the one who violates the law, there is nothing wrong with such a partnership. If the mudarib perishes and he has a debt, speculation funds, and deposits, and

<!-- page 51 -->
A concise book on the narrated reports, Volume Two

none of that is found in his particular estate — and it is not established against him that he consumed it, was compensated for it, destroyed it, mixed it with his own wealth, or did anything to make himself liable for it — then nothing is required of his heirs except an oath that they do not know he did any of those things, and the matter does not fall upon them even if the borrower and the holder of the debt and the depositor had priority. If any of these creditors dies, the claim goes to the one he named; if he did not hand it over, then the debt begins with him. If the funds are fully committed, it is not permissible to share them with an absent partner. If a man has a debt owed to him and no partnership was formed, nor was any valid company or mudaraba established — but the creditor prepares the debt and hands it to the debtor, who then participates in it or argues over it — that is permissible. If two partners separate and there remains a debt owed to them from their partnership, and they divide that debt on the condition that each of them recovers what came to him by the division — this is not permissible for them if they subsequently dispute it. What is necessary between them is to settle from what they jointly possess, and what remains of the debt comes from what they share. If one of them says to the other, "Give me my capital" — or whatever they had agreed upon — and says, "You have nothing beyond that, and you must make good whatever is lacking from it," and they agree to that, then it is permissible for them to act on what they have agreed.

**On Pre-emption:**

Pre-emption applies in the case of undivided shares and real estate — common shares or portions not yet divided between the seller and the partner or partners in it. The partner is the most deserving and the most entitled to the sale over the one who purchased it from a non-partner. If one of the partners exercises pre-emption without the rest, he is more entitled to it than those who did not exercise it. If they all exercise it, they share it equally, and each of them has his portion of the partnership, even if their shares differ in size; if they are equal, it is divided equally between them. There is no pre-emption for a neighbor or anyone else in divided property, nor is there pre-emption over a river, a ship, an animal, or anything other than real estate, undivided shares, land, and structures or trees growing on it. If something is owned jointly between two people and one of them sells his share, his partner is more entitled to it by pre-emption. Pre-emption applies on behalf of a child until he comes of age, and if his guardian or caretaker is in a position to exercise it on his behalf and it would benefit the child, pre-emption should be exercised for him. It also applies on behalf of the absent partner until he returns, or

<!-- page 52 -->


<!-- page 52 -->
The Book of Mukhtasar al-Asar — Volume Two

The intercessor is informed of the matter, and he either submits or allows the prayer between himself and the Sunnah. The one present must do what is required between the purchase and the passage of one year from that time, unless he submits the pre-emption and the intercession.

The intercessor of a partnership in a sale is obliged to take what is less or more of it, and whatever he is a partner in — whether all of it or part of it — such as a shared wall between two houses belonging to their owners, or one who has a common share or other rights therein. The sale falls upon him whether it is less or more, and intercession is thus obligatory for those who have a partnership in it, whether the amount is small or large.

Intercession is obligatory for the people of al-Raighah who do not have direct participation with one another, provided there is no barrier between them and the property in question. There is no intercession in a street that is commonly walked, because the people of the dwellings along that street do not own it as the people of al-Raighah own their private lane. Rather, they benefit from it through their courtyards, and passers-by on the road share in it. Therefore, the people of the street have no intercession in what is sold along it by right of the street — that right belongs to the people of al-Raighah among themselves.

If there is a house within al-Raighah with a dwelling, and that dwelling is sold, the people of the houses are more entitled to intercession than the buyer, and they may intercede for what is sold within al-Raighah. This is because they possess rights to their own courtyards, whereas the people of al-Raighah own nothing of those houses. Likewise, if there is a private lane within a lane, the people of the inner lane intercede for what lies within their path and passage. The people of the outer and upper portions of the lane do not intercede for what lies below, in what they have no right to and no path leading to — that which marks the boundaries of their dwellings within the lane — but this must be examined in light of shared rights, for the sharing of rights is taken into account.

Whoever has a right in what is sold, or in something among the rights upon which the sale falls, is a partner and therefore an intercessor, which reflects the principle of shared family rights. If he owns the house in its entirety, he has the right to open it from the outside and owns all of it, and no one has the right to speak to him about it or enter it without his permission. Similarly, if there is a lane within a lane, the owner of the inner lane possesses all rights to it from the street entrance up to its end, and he may restrict it as he wishes. As for what lies within al-Raighah — except what is bounded by a wall belonging to other people of the Raighah — it extends to its own boundary.

There is no intercession for a Jew or a Christian against a Muslim. Muslims may intercede in the sale of what they share with Muslims and dhimmis alike. Similarly, dhimmis intercede for one another in what they are bound to intercede for under the obligations of intercession. They have no intercession against Muslims in gifts,

<!-- page 53 -->
nor in charity, residence, life grants, neck grants, or intercession in what is sold together with an offer — such as a house sold together with its furnishings, an animal, or other goods. The intercessor is not required to say: "I will pay the value of that offer that accompanied the sale," and he takes the house accordingly.

Likewise, if a man marries a woman using a house as her dowry, and intercession is obligatory for the house, the intercessor does not have the right to say: "I will pay a dowry equivalent to this woman and take the house." However, if it is sold for a price and the price has been received, an offer is made to the intercessor to take it at the price at which the sale was concluded. Similarly, if the woman is married to the owner of the house with a known dowry and then that dowry is paid — this is equivalent to a sale — and the intercessor has the right to take the house with what the woman received as her dowry.

If what is obligatory for intercession is sold for a known equivalent, the intercessor may take it in exchange for that equivalent — such as if it is sold for wheat, dates, or something that is weighed and has a known equivalent. The intercession is then obligatory, and the intercessor is given that equivalent.

The intercessor may intercede on behalf of either the seller or the buyer, whichever the obligation falls upon. If the sale is concluded and completed between buyer and seller, and the intercessor rises against the seller before the price has been received from the buyer and pays it to him, the intercessor is not entitled to sell from that receipt. Nor may the buyer prevent him from doing so, nor say: "I will pay what I bought and take it from you, since the pre-emption has been established against me," because the intercessor's right rests upon the seller, and the buyer must deliver it.

If the buyer delays until he pays the price and then pre-emption is exercised within the obligatory period, that is valid. If what pre-emption is obligatory for has been taken by the buyer and he sells it and transfers ownership — whether or not the intercessor knows about it — the intercessor may intercede with whomever he chooses, paying that person whatever price he paid to the one from whom he purchased, whether it is more or less, provided the period of intercession has not expired and he has not relinquished it. Every sale that does not cancel his right of intercession obliges him to perform it unless the period has passed.

This is like a man who bought what was subject to intercession for another for a price, and then sold it to someone else for less or more, or another person sold it to yet another, and so a group of people came to own it in succession. If the intercessor wishes to intercede, he may do so with whomever he wishes, unless the period from the time of the original purchase has elapsed, or the intercessor has relinquished his right. If he rises against the first buyer and that buyer has sold it for more than he paid within the intercession period, then

<!-- page 54 -->
the intercession entails the return of the sale to the seller and what came after it, and the intercessor takes the property at the price the buyer paid, delivering it to him. The same applies to those who came after him.

Even if the same house has an upper and lower dwelling, and they are divided and sold separately, there is no intercession for the owner of the upper dwelling over the owner of the lower, or vice versa, if the division took place before the sale. If they remain stacked upon one another, then it is as with a neighbor relationship. If they share a common entrance or something that remained jointly held between them, and the sale was conducted on the seller's share in that common property, intercession is obligatory.

If a common-share property or a divisible property is sold in a single transaction, the partner in the jointly held portion has the right to take the entire transaction — both the common and the divided portions — and they are not permitted to take one part without the other, unless they agree. If the buyer purchases the common stock at a price and the sale is concluded between him and the seller, and then the seller frustrates the buyer from the price and the sale is concluded on what was received after the reversal, then if the intercessor arises and asks the buyer to take the first price, he is not entitled to that — he is only entitled to what was paid in the final price.

If the seller sells the property for a price and then gives some or all of that price back to the buyer as a gift or charity, and the intercessor then rises wishing to exercise intercession, he must pay the price at which the sale was made. He has no right to deduct what the seller gave back to the buyer — for that was done as a device and manipulation to cut off the intercession. If the seller inflates the price and then returns the surplus to the buyer, or the buyer pays part as profit and part as sale price in order to circumvent the intercessor through such a stratagem, the contract and the true intention are what govern, and the sale is only concluded on the whole price or on what was actually received — without gifts or charity invalidating the intercessor's right.

If the price is inflated and the intercessor wishes to proceed, he pays what the buyer actually paid and receives all that they agreed upon. This is proven and validated by the acknowledgment of the buyer upon which it is built. If it was in fact a matter of manipulation, and the intercessor claims so but has no evidence, while the other party denies it, the stronger obligation falls upon the one who denies it — he must take an oath to answer the intercessor's claim. Such manipulation to cut off the pre-emption is not permissible for either the seller or the buyer, because they are conspiring to invalidate one of the established rights.

If the sale is secret, the intercessor's right to intercede runs from the time the sale reaches his knowledge, for a period of one year — even if the sale was publicly known and the intercessor was present.

<!-- page 55 -->
If he delays until after the year has passed and then claims he was not informed of the sale, he is not believed in this — because the sale was known, and the obligation to intercede does not arise until the sale is concluded and finalized. Once it is concluded and the intercessor is bound by intercession, it is obligatory upon him even if the seller has not yet received the price, and the intercessor may pay whichever he chooses — the seller or the buyer.

If the intercessor goes against the buyer, the buyer must hand over the property to him and its custody, while the seller bears no custody obligation if the intercessor is appointed through him and the property is delivered from the seller.

If the buyer builds upon or sows what he has bought that is subject to intercession, that constitutes submission. If a man claims that he bought from an absent person, the intercessor may arise — however, neither the claim nor the pre-emption is established against the absent party. The intercessor may not exercise intercession until the sale is valid. Similarly, if the buyer claims to have purchased from a present person who denies it, the intercessor may not intercede until the sale is validated, and the intercession is valid from the time of that validation.

If a house is held in trust by certain notables, and some of them built within it and then died, leaving it to their heirs, and some heirs sold their inherited share — intercession is for the heirs. Whoever relinquished pre-emption before the sale and then the sale was conducted may do so or not; the obligation of relinquishment is not valid until after the sale is concluded.

If the intercessor and the buyer disagree about the price, the buyer's word is taken — with his oath — that he bought at the price he states, and the price at which he bought is paid, unless the intercessor offers him charity or takes an oath against him, in which case the price is paid to him. If it is later established that the buyer purchased for less than that, or he acknowledges it, the intercession belongs to the intercessor.

If the intercessor exercises intercession and then replaces the property and sells it, and then rises against the buyer demanding that the buyer pay him the price and give back what was bought from him and makes that his intercession — he is obliged to do that. If he does not do so, there is no obligation upon him.

This is all the position of the Ahl al-Bayt, may Allah's prayers be upon them all.

The Book of Mukhtasar al-Asar — Volume Two

Chapter on Oaths and Vows

<!-- page 58 -->
The Book of Mukhtasar al-Asar — Volume Two

<!-- page 59 -->
The Book of Mukhtasar al-Asar — Volume Two

On the Preservation of Oaths and Covenants:

Allah Almighty said: "And guard your oaths." (20) And those who are faithful to their oaths and covenants are shepherded. (21) He said: "Fulfill the covenant of Allah when you have covenanted, and do not break oaths after their confirmation." (22) He said: "Those who purchase with the covenant of Allah and their own oaths a small gain — those will have no share in the Hereafter, and Allah will not speak to them, nor look at them on the Day of Resurrection, nor will He purify them, and they will have a painful punishment." (23)

The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, said: He will give them charity and they will receive the torment of their hearts. He said: "Whoever swears an oath, let him swear by Allah, and whoever swears by Allah, let him believe in Allah; and if he does not do so, he is not a Muslim." And he said, peace be upon him: "Three persons whom Allah will not look at on the Day of Resurrection, will not purify, and for whom there is a painful punishment: a man who pledges allegiance to an imam for something of this world — if he is given it, he is loyal; if he is not, he withholds loyalty; a man who has water on the back of a road and withholds it from the traveler; and a man who swore after the afternoon prayer that he was given such-and-such goods, and the other believed him, when he was a liar."

Allah Almighty does not sanctify anyone who swears falsely in His name. And he, peace be upon him, said: "Beware of false oaths, for they deplete goods and remove blessings." Whoever dares to swear an oath by Allah, let him await His punishment. He said: "The one who breaks his pledge of allegiance will meet Allah on the Day of Resurrection in the most formidable state" — meaning he will have no hand.

On What Is and Is Not Binding in Oaths:

Allah Almighty said: "Allah will not take you to task for thoughtlessness in your oaths." (24) Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: Allah will not punish you for thoughtlessness in your oaths. The thoughtlessness referred to here is a person saying "No, by Allah" and "Yes, by Allah" without his heart being set on the oath. He, peace be upon him, means what a person says in the course of ordinary speech.

(20) Al-Ma'idah: 89
(21) Al-Mu'minun: 8
(22) Al-Nahl: 91
(23) Al Imran: 77
(24) Al-Baqarah: 225


<!-- page 60 -->
This is a book that is brief on the effects — Volume Two

His hadith concerns swearing by Allah when one does not actually intend to swear. This is confirmed by the saying of Allah Almighty: "Allah will not take you to task for what is vain in your oaths, but He will take you to task for what your hearts have earned." (25)

What matters is what the oath swore in his heart — what he truly intended. Ja'far ibn Muhammad clarified this by saying: there is a man who says something in the course of his speech that he did not do, and he did not make his conscience complicit in the oath. He said: "It is a vain utterance, so may Allah forgive it."

He also said: "To swear an oath on what the swearer truly intends."

It is reported that he forbade equivocation in oaths and said that if there is wrongdoing involved, then the intention of the one administering the oath — even if unjust — takes precedence over the intention of the swearer. Equivocation is the use of analogy in words, whereby one intends a thing but likens it to another, so that the listener is deceived into thinking that what is implied is what is meant, while in reality something else is intended. As for the People of the Book, they swear by Allah and by whatever they hold sacred in their own faith.

Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, commented on the words of Allah Almighty: "And remember your Lord if you forget" (26) — that if one says "I swear by Allah I will do such and such" and then forgets to say "if Allah wills," he should say "In sha' Allah" upon remembering. He also reported that a group of Jews came to the Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him, and asked him about something, and he said he would tell them the next day. He then waited for the revelation of Allah Almighty, and he had not made an exception in his statement. The revelation was delayed forty days, after which it was revealed to him, and thereafter it was said: "And never say of anything, 'I will do that tomorrow,' without adding, 'if Allah wills.'" (27)

They were commanded to include the exception in oaths, and the Commander of the Believers said, may Allah's prayers be upon him: whoever swears an oath and then says "In sha' Allah" has properly excepted.

Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers and peace be upon him, said: If he moves his tongue with the exception, he has made it, even if he does not say it aloud. He also reported that the Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, said: a man's oath has an exception, and the exception is valid if it is connected to the oath — that is, he swears and then says "if Allah wills" before he breaks off his speech. This is a permissible exception by consensus, and it does not obligate the one

(25) Al-Ma'idah: 89
(26) Al-Kahf: 24
(27) Al-Kahf: 23–24

<!-- page 61 -->
This is a book that is brief on the effects — Volume Two

who swore by it to exclude perjury. Ja'far ibn Muhammad said, may Allah's prayers be upon him, that the exception is permissible even after forty days, because the Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, made the exception after the revelation was withheld from him following his words to those who had asked him from among the Jews: "Tomorrow I will tell you." It is narrated from 'Ali, the Commander of the Faithful, may Allah's prayers be upon him, regarding the verse: "And remember your Lord if you forget."

Similarly, Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: the exception is permissible even after a year. The exception applies to an oath, specifically to what the swearer has sworn to do and then does not do. If a man swears an oath concerning a right he claims he will fulfill, he swears to the plaintiff, and the exception — whether made privately or publicly — does not apply in this regard. The oath in such a matter is governed purely by intention, and what is apparent is that the oath reflects the intention of the swearer. If the intention of the oath involves waiving a right, or fraud, or equivocation, then nothing is lifted from him by it; the oath and perjury both stand, and he must fulfill what he swore, or repent thereafter and seek forgiveness. There is no oath in that which the swearer has no capacity to fulfill — such as one who swears to free a slave he does not own, or to give something not in his possession. There is no oath of divorce, no manumission, no pilgrimage, no Umrah, and no oath sworn by anything other than Allah, Blessed and Exalted.

Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him, was asked about the words of Allah Almighty: "The Prophet confided to one of his wives a matter in confidence" (29) — to the end of the verse. The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, had confided to one of his wives — the Coptic Mariyah — at the beginning of what had occurred between them: he had come to her and embraced her in a house, and 'A'ishah observed this. She asked him about it, and he, wishing to please her, swore that he would not return to Mariyah. He then forbade her to himself and instructed his wife to keep the matter quiet. She did not do so, and Hafsah disclosed it to others. So Allah Almighty sent down revelation to him, (30) and commanded him to expiate the oath he had sworn.

(28) Al-Kahf: 24
(29) Al-Tahrim: 3
(30) Al-Tahrim: 1
(31) Al-Tahrim: 5

<!-- page 62 -->
Volume Two — This is a book that is brief on the effects

He was not blamed for forbidding what was halal to himself, but he was rebuked for it. Whatever Allah Almighty has made lawful for him has not been forbidden to him; it remains halal for him, and he should fulfill it. There is nothing upon him, even if the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, said: "Whoever swears an oath on a matter and then sees that something contrary to what he swore is better for him, let him expiate his oath and do what is better." Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: every oath sworn in the commission of a sin carries nothing — no perjury and no expiation. Whoever swears not to do what is obligatory upon him, or not to do what is permissible for him, and then refrains, has committed no wrong, and there is neither perjury nor expiation upon him. Whoever swears in obedience to do something must do what he has sworn; if he does not, he must expiate his oath.

**Mentioning Vows:**

Allah Almighty said: "The righteous will drink from a cup whose mixture is camphor." (32)

The Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, was asked — up to the words of the Almighty, "They fulfill their vows and fear a day whose evil is widespread" (33) — about vows made to other than Allah, Blessed and Exalted, and about vows made in disobedience.

Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: this is like a man who vows that if he is able to become intoxicated he will drink, or that he will commit adultery, or theft, or murder — such things are forbidden to him. Even if he binds himself to these things through a vow or an oath, or anything else, he must not do any of them, nor does perjury or any oath obligation fall upon him for abandoning them. He must not make a vow involving the severing of kinship ties. Whoever makes a vow in obedience to Allah or to give thanks for a blessing must fulfill it — such as saying: "If such and such occurs, I must fulfill my vow." This includes saying: "If I do not perform such and such prayer, or do such and such good deed — freeing a slave, giving charity, fasting, or any other act he has named or mentioned — then I am bound to do that good which he has made obligatory upon himself, or to fulfill the vow he has made." Or he may say: "If Allah cures me

(32) Al-Insan: 5
(33) Al-Insan: 7

<!-- page 63 -->
This is a book that is brief on the effects — Volume Two

of my illness," or "if He returns me safely from my journey," or "if He grants me wealth or a son" — and he names such and such a vow — then he must fulfill it. Likewise, if Allah Almighty does for him what he had hoped and made a vow upon himself in, he must fulfill it even if he did not name the vow specifically. However, if he said: "If such and such comes to pass from what I have mentioned, then I make a vow to Allah" — but did not specify that vow — then nothing is required of him. If he vows something and receives it, fulfilling the vow is voluntary, not obligatory upon him. If he makes a vow resembling the expiation of an oath, that is itself a good deed.

**Mentioning Expiations:**

Allah Almighty said: "Allah will not take you to task for vain oaths, but He will take you to task for what your hearts have earned. The expiation is the feeding of ten poor persons from the middle of what you feed your own families, or clothing them, or freeing a slave. Whoever cannot find any of these must fast for three days. That is the expiation for your oaths when you have sworn them; and guard your oaths." (34)

Whoever swears by Allah and then commits perjury, it is obligatory upon him — "Allah makes clear to you His signs, that you may be grateful."

The expiation for him is from the above: if he is able, he feeds ten poor persons, or clothes them, or frees a slave. He is free to choose whichever of these he wishes, since he is capable of any of them.

Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: regarding everything in the Qur'an stated as "or" — giving the choice between options — if he cannot find one or is unable to do it, he must do the first if and when he finds it and is able; the second does not suffice for him unless he cannot find the first or is incapable of it. If the one who swore cannot find anything with which to feed ten poor persons, and has neither small nor large means to free or clothe ten poor persons, he must fast for three days. Fasting does not take the place of feeding until none of what has been mentioned is available — neither food, nor clothing, nor manumission. The feeding in expiation for an oath does not satisfy the poor through a single day's provision only; the minimum is a supply of food sufficient to sustain them. A middle level would be vinegar and oil; meat is a higher level; and salt alone is the minimum, which does not suffice. This is based on the words of Allah Almighty: "from the middle of what you feed your families" (35) — and this middle level is what he must provide in expiation for the oath. If he feeds them something better, that is preferable. As for clothing

(34) Al-Ma'idah: 89
(35) Al-Ma'idah: 89

<!-- page 64 -->
Volume Two — This is a book that is brief on the effects

in the expiation of the oath, each poor person is to receive two garments. In the expiation of the oath, the expiation known as zihar expiation may not be fulfilled by freeing a mukatab slave unless he returns to his original slave status and is then freed. Fasting in the expiation of the oath is three consecutive days. Feeding and clothing the people of prescribed categories from the expiation of the oath applies to those who are so commanded. If such persons are not found, then the weak who cannot support themselves are entitled. One must not give to a Nasib — one known for enmity toward the family of Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him and his family — anything from expiation, charity, or any other religious disbursement, even if no one else can be found.

Whoever makes a vow concerning something that had already come to pass before he made it upon himself, and he did not know this, then nothing is required of him. A man asked Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him, saying: "I have a slave girl. I dislike having a child by her, and her menstruation became irregular, and she feared she might be pregnant. So she made a vow to Allah — fasting and charity — if she menstruated. She then menstruated before I knew anything of this." He said: nothing is required.

Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: if a man swears an oath not to give another man dinars in exchange for goods he has not specified, and he then gives them, he has not committed perjury. If he swears not to sell his goods at a stated price and then sells them at an equal price without specifying it, he has not committed perjury. If a man is owed dinars and swears he will not accept them in full, but is then given part in coin and part in goods of equal value, he has not committed perjury.

He was asked about a man who was in a state of ihram and slaughtered his son's animal, and he said: there is nothing wrong with that, and it is among the steps of the devil. He also said, may Allah's prayers be upon him: expiation comes after perjury.

**Chapter on Food**

<!-- page 66 -->
This is a book that is brief on the effects — Volume Two
