Her husband's own Islam preserves their marriage, and those who own them may separate them if they wish, as is the case with all their servants — except that those in captivity are entrusted with a bond that is not to be severed between them, and that is their right, so separation is not obligatory.

Ali, peace be upon him, said: it is not permissible for Muslims to marry polytheist women in the abode of war if the rulings of the polytheists apply to them.

It was narrated from him, peace be upon him, that he said: if a woman from the people of war comes out to the House of Islam and she has a polytheistic husband, his bond to her is severed and she may marry after her womb is cleared, if she wishes. If her husband comes as a Muslim and is trustworthy, he has no recourse to her except by a renewed marriage, because when she went out to the House of Islam her bond to him was severed.

If a man accepts Islam and he has a polytheistic wife, she remains his wife. If a woman accepts Islam and her husband is a polytheist, a separation is made between them. If her husband accepts Islam before her waiting period has ended, they remain bound to one another; if her waiting period has expired, she owns herself.

From Ali and Abu Ja'far, peace be upon them both: they forbade the marriage of Arab Christian women and the eating of their sacrificial animals.

It was narrated from Ali, peace be upon him, that he said: an apostate who apostasizes and joins the abode of war is like a dead man — his wealth is divided among his heirs and his wives observe the waiting period of widows; they may then marry if they wish.

From Ja'far ibn Muhammad, peace be upon them both:


<!-- page 160 -->
If a woman embraces Islam while her husband remains a polytheist, she is separated from him. If he does not submit to Islam, she retains her full dower; if he refuses to enter Islam, she is entitled to half of it. If polytheists embrace Islam, they remain upon their existing marriages. The Messenger of Allah (peace be upon him) said: "Accept the people of the pre-Islamic era upon what they embraced Islam — whether in matters of marriage, divorce, or inheritance." It was narrated from 'Ali (may Allah's prayers be upon him) that he said regarding the polytheist who embraces Islam while having two free sisters as wives, or more than four free wives: leave him to marry the first of the two sisters and to separate from the second, and to separate from whichever wives he took after the permitted four. It was narrated from him that he said: "Allah made permissible the women of the People of the Book for Muslim men when Muslim women were few, but when Muslim women became many, Allah Almighty said: 'And do not marry polytheist women.'" (101) And He said: "And do not hold fast to the bonds of unbelieving women." (102) The Messenger of Allah (peace be upon him and his family) forbade a Muslim man from marrying a non-Muslim woman when he can find a Muslim woman, and forbade a Muslim woman from marrying a polytheist. If a polytheist embraces Islam while having a polytheist wife, he may keep her with him in the hope that Allah Almighty will guide her, and he may marry Muslim women — up to three additional wives — if he is aware of his situation. If he marries a Muslim woman while still having a polytheist wife, and the Muslim woman does not know of this, then when she discovers it after consummation, the matter is placed in her hands: if she wishes to remain with him, she may do so, and if she wishes to leave, she may do so. She is entitled to what is lawfully owed to her, and she may marry whomever she wishes once her waiting period has expired, without requiring a divorce. If the polytheist wife is divorced before her 'iddah expires from the day the Muslim wife makes her choice, then the Muslim wife has greater right to remain.

**Mention of the Division Concerning Harm Among Wives:**

Allah Almighty said: "You will never be able to be perfectly just between wives even if you so desire; so do not incline altogether, leaving the other as if suspended." (103) This indicates that justice between wives in matters of desire, passion, and physical inclination is impossible except in the case of intercourse, whereas in matters of maintenance, lodging, and gifts

(101) Al-Baqarah: 221
(102) Al-Mumtahanah: 10
(103) Al-Nisa': 129

<!-- page 161 -->
and the like, a man is capable of exercising justice. If a man has multiple wives, it is obligatory upon him to spend each day and night equitably with each of them. Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's prayers be upon him) was asked about a man who has wives who feel jealousy of one another, and he said: he must spend a night with each of them — one night each — remaining with her. If he is not capable of intercourse, that is not held against him. If a man is capable of intercourse with some of his wives, he must not withhold this from the others. It was narrated from 'Ali (may Allah's prayers be upon him) that he was asked about a man who has wives and then departs on a journey; he said: when he departs, he should begin with the one whose night is due. If a man has only one wife, he must not leave her without spending the night with her for more than four nights. He may do in the remaining three nights what Allah has made lawful for him. Ja'far ibn Muhammad said: if a man has two wives, he may single out one of them for three nights, which is her due, and divide the remaining night between them. If there are three wives, each receives her night. If there are four, he may single out whichever of them he wishes, and if there are four, he may not prefer one over another. It was narrated from 'Ali (may Allah's prayers be upon him) that he said concerning a man who has a wife or several wives and then marries again: if he marries a virgin, he stays with her for seven nights; if he marries a previously married woman, he stays with her for three nights. He then divides the nights equitably among all his wives. He was asked about the words of Allah Almighty: "If a woman fears from her husband ill-treatment or desertion, there is no blame on them if they reach a reconciliation between themselves." (104) He said: this concerns a man who has two wives and becomes incapacitated regarding one of them, or she is a dhimmī, so he inclines toward the other and wishes to divorce the first, while she dislikes that. She may therefore reconcile with him by permitting him to visit her only occasionally, or by yielding her turn to the other.

**Mention of Expenditure upon Wives:**

Allah Almighty said: "We have prescribed for them regarding their wives and those whom their right hands possess." (105) And He said: "Provide for them and clothe them in a fitting manner," (106) and the word of the dowry is mentioned.

(104) Al-Nisa': 128
(105) Al-Ahzab: 50

<!-- page 162 -->
From the family of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him), it is narrated that he addressed the people in the Farewell Sermon, mentioned women, and commended them with good advice, saying: "They are entitled to their sustenance and clothing in a fitting manner." He forbade (peace be upon him) that a man be satisfied while his family goes hungry, and said: "It is sufficient sin for a person to neglect those who depend upon him." It is narrated from him (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) that he said: "There are seven precedent deeds you must fulfill," and he mentioned among them maintenance for children. It is narrated from him (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) that he said: "Effort in the face of hardship, the burden of many children, scarce wealth — these are among the two heavy burdens." It is narrated from him (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) that he said: "Whoever is frugal in his livelihood, Allah provides for him; and whoever is wasteful, Allah withholds from him." It is narrated from him (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) that he said: "When Allah wills good for the people of a household, He grants them sound understanding in religion, provides for them with gentleness in their livelihood, and grants them discernment in their affairs." It is narrated from him (may Allah's prayers be upon him and his family) that economy is half of one's livelihood. It is narrated from him (may Allah's prayers be upon him) that if a man finds nothing to spend on his wife and brings her something that barely sustains her, he is not to be separated from her even if he finds nothing at all. It is narrated with his prayers that if a woman refuses to allow her husband to spend on her harm, he is imprisoned at her expense. He said: "Any woman who leaves her husband's house without his permission is entitled to no maintenance until she returns." It was narrated from him that he said concerning a man who divorces his wife while she is nursing his child: she is to be given the wage of a nursing woman, and if her husband dies and leaves her with a son whom she nurses, she is entitled to a wage commensurate with what is taken from the boy's wealth — from inheritance or otherwise — and the guardian has no right to remove the child from her lap. As for expenditure upon wives and those relatives upon whom it is obligatory, it is according to sufficiency and means. Allah Almighty said: "Let a man of means spend from his means." (107) Thus the ruler imposes upon those obligated to spend an amount appropriate to their standing, without excess or deficiency. Allah Almighty said: "And those who, when they spend, are neither extravagant nor miserly, but hold a just balance between those extremes." (108)

(106) Al-Nisa': 5
(107) Al-Talaq: 7
(108) Al-Furqan: 67

<!-- page 163 -->
This is something known and not unknown, and there is no need for personal opinion or ijtihad regarding it — its standard is known to people from their circumstances in every place and time, and according to prices and commodities, the prevailing market rates, high prices or low prices, poverty and wealth. Every person who is poor and unable to work or earn is required to spend on those who would inherit from him if he died, in proportion to what they would inherit from his estate — except the wife, for whom the husband must spend even if he is among those obliged to spend on others. If he is poor and has nothing to spend, the situation of the one upon whom spending is obligatory is like the situation of one who has no guardian: Allah, glorified and exalted be He, provides for them. He has no obligation to provide for those who would not inherit from him if he were to die, if his heirs are poor and have no wealth, because his expenditure is obligatory upon him and Allah has excused him. Allah Almighty said: "Allah does not burden a soul beyond its capacity," (109) and He said: "Allah does not burden it." (110) The easiest course thereafter is that spending be on what is present, and there is no right to demand it for the past.

**Mention of al-Istibra':**

We have narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) that a man from the Ansar invited the Messenger of Allah (peace be upon him) to a meal, and he accepted the invitation. The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) said to him: "A child of mine whom I purchased a few days ago — she was pregnant." He said: "Perhaps you were intimate with her?" He said: "Yes, O Messenger of Allah, I did." He said: "The Messenger of Allah — were it not for the sanctity of your food, I would curse you. You have entered into her womb; your semen has nourished its hearing, its sight, its flesh, its blood, its hair, and its skin." He said (peace be upon him): "It is not permissible for a man to approach a pregnant woman belonging to another until she delivers what is in her womb. And if he purchases a newborn woman who is pregnant, he must not approach her until she gives birth and becomes pure." It is narrated from him (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) that the slave woman is absolved of obligation if a man bends toward her during her menstruation.

(109) Al-Baqarah: 286
(110) Al-Talaq: 7

<!-- page 164 -->
'Ali (peace be upon him) said: Whoever buys a young slave girl who has not yet reached puberty and has shown no signs of it need not fear the obligation of istibra' upon her; he may approach her if he wishes and is not required to perform istibra'. He also said: Whoever buys a slave girl who has despaired of menstruation should isolate her for two months and then approach her if he fears she may menstruate. If her menstruation has ceased, Abu 'Abdullah Ja'far ibn Muhammad said: if he buys an old woman and is certain she does not menstruate and there is no doubt about her, he may approach her if he wishes. He said: Whoever buys a newborn woman — it is permissible for him to approach her before performing istibra', though if he performs istibra' as a precaution it is better. However, istibra' is obligatory upon the seller if he approaches the slave woman and then wishes to sell her — he must not sell her until he has performed istibra' from menstruation, lest the lineage of his son be cast into doubt. It was narrated from 'Ali (may Allah's prayers be upon him) that he said: if a man buys a slave girl who does not menstruate or has reached puberty without menstruating, he must perform istibra' upon her for forty-five nights. He was asked about a man who bought a slave girl who had reached the age of dreams and had not conceived a child, then took her and sold her, and he said (peace be upon him): "This is a serious matter — he must perform istibra' upon her for nine months, then approach her after nine months if no pregnancy appears, and take precaution." He said (peace be upon him) that a man purchases a slave girl while she is in a state of purity, and the seller informs him that he did not approach her since her last menstruation or during the purity in which she currently is — he said (peace be upon him): if he trusts him, he may approach her if he wishes, and if he performs istibra' it is more cautious for him. He said (peace be upon him): Whoever buys a slave girl who has had no husband and whose master has not approached her must perform istibra' upon her by one menstrual cycle. He was asked (peace be upon him) about a man who bought a slave girl, then freed her and married her — it was narrated from Ja'far ibn Muhammad (peace be upon him) that he had mercy shown to him during her menstruation. And if he approached her before performing istibra', there is no harm in that. He (peace be upon him) was asked about a man who contracted with his slave woman and then wished to marry her before performing istibra' — he said: No. It was said to him: "How much istibra' should he perform?" He said: "One or two menstrual cycles is more beloved to me." A man must perform istibra' if he wishes to sell the slave woman; he performs istibra' when he purchases her. It is narrated from 'Ali (may Allah's prayers be upon him) that he said: if a man buys a slave woman, it is permissible for him to be affectionate with her before performing istibra' — meaning touching, kissing, and intimacy short of intercourse. It was narrated from him that he was asked about a man who purchased a woman and feared she might be pregnant — he said: he performs istibra' upon her for forty-five nights.

<!-- page 165 -->
He (peace be upon him) was asked about a slave woman who committed adultery — is it permissible for her master to approach her? He said: He does not approach her until he has performed istibra' upon her. If a man commits adultery with a slave woman and then purchases her, he must not approach her until he has performed istibra' upon her. If she conceived by him through adultery, the child is not attributed to him, for the Messenger of Allah said: "The child belongs to the bed, and for the fornicator is the stone." It was narrated from Ja'far ibn Muhammad (peace be upon him) that he said: Whoever buys a slave girl while she is menstruating — when she becomes pure, let him approach her if he wishes. It was narrated from 'Ali (peace be upon him) that he said: a woman taken captive who has a husband — she performs istibra' by one menstrual cycle, and when she becomes pure, her new master may approach her, and her former husband may not approach her until he has performed istibra' upon her by one menstrual cycle.

<!-- page 166 -->

<!-- page 167 -->
**Chapters of Divorce**

<!-- page 168 -->


<!-- page 169 -->
Allah Almighty said: "O you who divorce women, divorce them at the start of their waiting periods and count the 'iddah." (111) He also said: "And if they separate, Allah will enrich each of them from His abundance." (112)

The Almighty makes clear that divorce is mentioned in His Book and that it rests in the hands of men, not women, so a man may divorce his wife whenever he wishes, for a reason or without one. It has been narrated from 'Ali (peace be upon him) that he disliked divorce without cause, though he did not forbid it, nor did he invalidate a divorce pronounced without reason. As Umm Sa'id related: one day she was pouring water over his hands, peace be upon him, and he raised his head and said, "Umm Sa'id." She replied, "At your service, O Commander of the Faithful." He said, "I long to take a bride." She said, "And what prevents the Commander of the Faithful?" He replied, "After four days in al-Raḥbah," and he then divorced one wife and took another in her place.

Divorce is an ugly act and I dislike it. Al-Hasan (peace be upon him) divorced many women without cause. It was said that he once passed by a group of women who greeted him, and he acknowledged them. He then remarked, "There is not one among us who has not married her and then divorced her." What this most conveyed was that the people of Kufa do not hold marriage in proper esteem — a man in the absolute sense.

**Mentioning the divorce that is forbidden and that which is permissible:**

We have narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family), and from the Imams of his household, that 'Abdullah ibn 'Umar divorced his wife three times while she was menstruating. He was told: let him take her back, then divorce her according to the Sunnah if he wishes. Whoever divorces his wife after having consummated the marriage — while she is in a state of purity in which he has been intimate with her, or while she is menstruating, or he divorces her without calling witnesses to the divorce as Allah Almighty commanded, or he divorces her while she is in a state of impurity from blood, or he swears an oath of divorce and commits perjury — his divorce is not a valid divorce and she remains his wife. This is bid'ah divorce, because it contradicts what Allah Almighty has commanded and the Sunnah of His Messenger (peace be upon him and his family) regarding divorce according to the Sunnah. Both divorces are nonetheless equivalent in form even if their wording differs, because a man who wishes to divorce his wife should wait, if she is among those who menstruate, until she menstruates and then becomes pure.

(111) Divorce: 1
(112) Al-Nisa': 130

<!-- page 170 -->
Once she has been cleansed of blood and has purified herself from menstruation, he divorces her before touching her, while she is in a state of purity in which she has not been touched — one divorce — and he calls two witnesses of just character to this, as Allah Almighty has commanded. He retains the right to take her back and may do so if he wishes, and between them they will have two divorces remaining. She then menstruates three full menstrual cycles, and when she has completed three cycles, she owns herself and he has no right of return over her unless both he and she desire it, whereupon he may marry her anew with a guardian, witnesses, and a dowry, and he will still have two divorces remaining over her. This is the Sunnah divorce, and it is recommended, because Allah Almighty says: "You do not know — perhaps Allah will bring about something after that." (113) That is, the possibility of taking her back. If he then divorces her — as we first described — one divorce, and then takes her back before her waiting period expires, or after it has ended he marries her anew, he may not divorce her again until he has been intimate with her. If he then wishes to divorce her again, he waits until she menstruates and purifies herself and divorces her while she is in a state of purity in which he has not touched her, calling just witnesses. If he wishes to take her back again, he does so as we described before, before her waiting period expires. If her waiting period has expired, she owns herself, and if both parties are willing, he may marry her again as we described. If he takes her back or marries her anew, she is his wife and remains with him with one divorce remaining. If he wishes to divorce her, he may not do so until he has been intimate with her, then waits until she menstruates and purifies herself, then divorces her while she is in a state of purity in which he has not touched her — a third divorce — and she then becomes irrevocably separated from him; it is not lawful for him to remarry her unless she has first been married to another husband, as Allah Almighty has said. This is the divorce of the 'iddah: if he divorces her and then takes her back in reality, then divorces her three times as we have described, this is the correct and permissible divorce about which there is no disagreement.

If he divorces her two or three times simultaneously, even one of these is not permissible; for if he pronounces one divorce upon her she becomes divorced, and a second divorce does not take effect upon her until he takes her back and she is again his wife — for whoever divorces a woman who is already divorced, that divorce is not valid and does not take effect unless she is his wife. This is analogous to someone divorcing a woman who is not his wife without her husband's authorization: that divorce is not valid. Likewise, if he divorces her three times at once, or whatever number he names, whether while she is menstruating, or in a state of purity in which he has been intimate with her, or without witnesses —

(113) Divorce: 1

<!-- page 171 -->
that divorce is not permissible, because it contradicts what Allah Almighty has commanded regarding divorce and the Sunnah of His Messenger (peace be upon him and his family). It was also said that those who permit this form of divorce and call it the Sunnah of the general Muslim community have contradicted both the Qur'an and the Sunnah, and the one who pronounces such a divorce has committed a transgression — yet they claimed that the divorce takes effect and is binding.

It was said to them: What do you say if a man marries a woman contrary to the Qur'an and Sunnah — for instance, if he marries her while she is in her 'iddah? When asked whether they permit marriage contrary to the Qur'an and Sunnah, they say that such a marriage is not permissible and forbid it, yet they have no argument for their position. So it is said to them: this divorce, which you have permitted despite its being contrary to the Qur'an and Sunnah, is analogous to a marriage contrary to the Qur'an and Sunnah, which you yourselves have denied as permissible — for this divorce forbids this woman to the one who divorced her and makes her lawful to another. If this divorce is not valid, then that marriage is likewise not valid, because it was this husband's annulment of her marriage through this divorce that was taken to establish her as the wife of a second husband. There is no analysis of the dissolution and reconstitution of marriage outside the Qur'an and Sunnah, and you are dismissing that. The arguments on this parable extend beyond the scope of this book.

It is not permissible for women to be witnesses to divorce, because Allah Almighty said: "And call to witness two men of justice from among you." (114) Divorce applies in five categories of women, who are divorced in all circumstances without waiting for menstruation or purification: the pregnant woman whose pregnancy may become apparent; the child who has not yet reached the age of menstruation; the virgin who has never menstruated; the woman who is past menstruation; the woman whose husband has not yet been intimate with her and whose husband is absent from her far away. When one of these women is divorced, the divorce takes effect upon her. She counts every month in place of a menstrual cycle, except for the one whose husband has not been intimate with her, for if he divorces her she has no 'iddah, according to the words of Allah Almighty: "You have no 'iddah to observe toward them." (115)

**Mentioning metaphors for divorce and the word divorce:**

If one who wishes to divorce his wife says to her, "You are divorced," or says, "She is divorced" and calls her by her name, or calls her and says, "She is divorced," or says he has given her over intending thereby divorce, or sends a messenger with that, or says that so-and-so has no metaphor of divorce and there is no word for it beyond these two —

(114) Al-Aḥzab: 49

<!-- page 172 -->
— these two formulations, because they are what Allah Almighty has mentioned in His Book: "O Prophet, when you divorce women." (115) He named nothing but divorce and 'iddah. If a man says to his wife, "You are confined," "a wilderness," "barren," "a morsel," "forbidden," or anything else other than divorce and 'iddah, it means nothing — just as if he says to her other things besides divorce. If he says to her, "You are forbidden to me," or "your release is forbidden," or something of that nature which he has imposed upon himself, it has no legal consequence.

Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali (peace be upon him) was informed of a man who said to his wife, "You are forbidden to me." He said: "If I had authority over him I would have struck his head, for Allah will resolve it for you — He will make it permissible, because he went no further than lying. He claimed that what Allah has made lawful for him is forbidden to him, yet it is not forbidden to him. Divorce is not obligatory for him, nor does he owe expiation." As was said, because Allah Almighty said: "O Prophet, why do you forbid what Allah has made lawful for you?" (116) And He placed in it only his disavowal, and this confirms what we have said.

For Allah Almighty rebuked His Prophet (peace be upon him and his family) but made expiation for him — he had sworn by Allah and said, "I have no partner in this; Allah has made lawful for you the sweetness of what your right hand possesses." It has been narrated that the Messenger of Allah (peace be upon him and his family) had entrusted the Coptic Mariyah to his household before Ibrahim (peace be upon him) was born of her. 'A'ishah came to her and he wished to please her, so he forbade Mariyah to himself and swore an oath to her that he would not approach her after that, and he confided it as a secret to her, but she disclosed it to Hafsah. This became apparent to the Messenger of Allah (peace be upon him and his family), and so Allah Almighty revealed: "O Prophet, why do you forbid what Allah has made lawful for you, seeking the approval of your wives? And Allah is Forgiving, Merciful. Allah has already ordained for you the dissolution of your oaths, and Allah is your Protector, and He is the All-Knowing, the All-Wise. And when the Prophet confided a matter to one of his wives, and when she disclosed it — and Allah informed him of it — he made known part of it and overlooked part. And when he told her of it, she said: Who told you this? He said: The All-Knowing, the All-Aware told me." (117)

(115) Divorce: 1
(116) Prohibition: 1
(117) Prohibition: 2

<!-- page 173 -->
In accordance with this, Jibrīl and the believers are his supporters, and after that, if he divorces you, his Lord will replace you with wives better than you — obedient, believing, devoutly worshipping, repentant, worshipping, given to fasting, previously married and virgins. (118) So He commanded him to expiate the oath he had sworn, he disavowed it and returned to her, and Ibrahim was born of her, a son to him (peace be upon him).

**Mentioning the option and ownership:**

We have narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family), and from the Imams of his household, that his wives (peace be upon him and his family) showed discontent with him and asked him for more of the goods of this world, as women were wont to do, saying to him, "If we were divorced, we would find among our people those who are more prosperous." The Messenger of Allah (peace be upon him and his family) was troubled by this and withdrew from them, and the revelation was withheld for twenty days. Then Allah Almighty revealed: "O Prophet, say to your wives: If you desire the life of this world and its adornment, then come — I will provide for you and release you graciously. But if you desire Allah, His Messenger, and the abode of the Hereafter, then indeed Allah has prepared for the doers of good among you a great reward." (119) He then withdrew from them all for twenty-nine nights in the house of Umm Ibrahim, and he called them and presented to them what Allah had revealed concerning them and their welfare, and gave them the choice. Peace be upon him and his family.

Abu Ja'far and Abu 'Abdillah (peace be upon them) said: it was not a divorce — had they chosen themselves it would have been one clear divorce. Abu 'Abdillah (peace be upon him) also said: if a man wishes to give his wife the option, let him withdraw from her as the Messenger of Allah (peace be upon him and his family) did, until she menstruates and then purifies herself, and then give her the option while she is in a state of purity in which he has not touched her, calling witnesses to that. If she then chooses herself, she is divorced and he has no right of return over her; he may then propose to her if he wishes to marry her by proper courtship. If she chooses him, it is not a divorce. The option is expressed by saying, "I have chosen myself." If she chooses herself or says, "I have chosen you" — if she chose him — and she remained silent until she rose or departed from

(118) Prohibition: 1–5
(119) Al-Aḥzab: 28–29


<!-- page 174 -->
In its place they parted, for the option that had been made for him was cut off, even if he returned to the option before she chose.

She has no right to choose, and if he stands up toward her, puts his hand on her, kisses her, or draws her close before she speaks, the choice that was made for her is cut off. And if it was good for her, she said, "I do not accept your choice," she had no choice.

If he chooses her while she is menstruating, or in a state of purity in which he has touched her, or if two witnesses do not testify, then this does not count. He said: "There is no divorce, no khul'ah, no match, and no choice except with the testimony of two witnesses, in a state of purity without intercourse."

He said: "If a man chooses his wife, or her father, or her brother chooses on her behalf, and she is satisfied with the choice of the one who makes the choice for her, then it is permissible." Similarly, ownership is like a choice — that is, to say to her, "You have sovereignty over yourself," or "Your queen commands you," for it is as if he said to her, "Choose me."

Mentioning what constitutes a valid divorce and what does not:

We have narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) that there is no divorce except after marriage, and no divorce for one who has not married. He said: "There is no divorce until after marriage, and no emancipation until after ownership, and no joining in fasting or silence from day to night, no breastfeeding after weaning, and no orphan status after a dream."

A man asked Abu Ja'far ibn Muhammad (peace be upon him) and said that he had a relative who had been proposed to him and he wanted to marry her, and that before that day he had said, "If I marry her, she is divorced." He said: "She was not married." He said: "No." He said: "Then marry her if you wish, for your divorce does not apply if you do not have her bound to you by marriage." And Abu 'Abdullah Ja'far ibn Muhammad (peace be upon him) was asked about the man who says, "If I marry so-and-so, or marry in such-and-such a land, or buy so-and-so, or go somewhere in secret, or buy such-and-such, then the one I marry is divorced and what I bought is charity." He said: "This is not so. All of this is something not permissible — to divorce what is not yet in one's bond, nor to manumit, nor to give in charity, unless he owns it."

He was asked about a man who divorced his wife while he was ill and her waiting period expired — did she or did she not forfeit her right? One of them died, and he said: "If her 'iddah expires while he is ill or while he is healthy, she does not inherit from him. And if he dies of his illness after her 'iddah has expired, or before it has ended unless the divorce was valid, she does not inherit." And from 'Ali (peace be upon him):

<!-- page 175 -->
He said: Every divorce is permissible — meaning it is obligatory for the sane person — but Abu Ja'far and Abu 'Abdullah (peace be upon them) both said that the one who has lost his mind is not permitted to divorce, and no divorce counts from him.

He was asked about the man who saw in his dream that he had divorced his wife. He said: "This is from the devil, and the divorce of a sleeping person counts for nothing, even if his tongue utters it while he is asleep. He wakes up and says what he should say, and it is not permissible for a person without reason, nor a delirious person, nor one who is possessed, nor a boy who has not yet reached puberty, to divorce. And the divorce of a drunken man who does not comprehend does not count."

From Ja'far ibn Muhammad (peace be upon him) he said: "Whoever is compelled to divorce his wife — his divorce is not valid, and it is not permissible to divorce under compulsion."

He said: "The man who says to his wife, 'Your divorce is upon you one and a half times' — it is one divorce," he said (peace be upon him), "and divorce does not admit of fractions." And if he says to her, "You are divorced three times, a quarter, a sixth, or some named or unnamed fraction of a divorce," then it is one divorce. If he says, "There is no divorce for one, then for all of them," what is permissible for divorce applies. And if he says, "I divorce you one divorce shared between you" — each of them is divorced once. Similarly if he says, "Your divorce is upon all of you, two divorces between you, or three, or four" — each of them is divorced once. If he says to them, "I divorce you five divorces shared between you, each of them is not obligated except for one divorce" — and this is based on the principle we have presented, that only one divorce in a state of purity is valid, and if he exceeds it, it counts for nothing.

Mentioning khul'ah and mubarat:

Allah the Almighty said: "And it is not permissible for you to take anything from what you have given them, except when both fear that they will not keep the limits set by Allah. If you fear that they will not keep the limits of Allah, then there is no sin upon either of them regarding what she gives to redeem herself." (120) We have narrated from the Imams of the family of the Prophet of Allah (peace be upon him and his family), from 'Ali (peace be upon him) and the Imams from his sons, that khul'ah is permissible when a man is placed in the proper circumstance — that is, if she says to her husband, "I fear that I will not maintain the limits of Allah in regard to you, so I give you such-and-such and release me," or her husband says something to that effect. It is permissible for them to carry out what they have agreed upon.

(120) Al-Baqarah: 229

<!-- page 176 -->
He has no right to take anything from her unless she transgresses in her words and says to her husband: "I do not obey your command, I will not swear by you, I will not wash away janabah for you, nor will I give you the rights of your bed that you are entitled to, or I will allow someone to enter upon you without your permission," or she says, "I will not maintain the limits of Allah in regard to you." If it comes to that, then it is permissible for him to take what she has ransomed herself with, even beyond what he gave her. But if she does not say that, he may not take from her more than what he gave her.

He said: "It is not khul'ah and it is not mubarat except when done like a divorce — in a state of purity in which he has not touched her, and in the presence of witnesses." And if he divorces her for that, he divorces her one divorce, as we have described above. It becomes clear from him that she is released from him, and he has no recourse to her unless she wishes to return. They may reconcile by resuming the marriage, and he retains what remains of her divorce count. He may marry her if he wishes and she wishes, before her waiting period expires and after it.

As for the words of Allah the Almighty: "And if you fear a breach between the two, send an arbitrator from his family and an arbitrator from her family. If they both wish for reconciliation, Allah will bring agreement between them. Indeed, Allah is All-Knowing, All-Aware." (121)

It was narrated from 'Ali, Abu Ja'far, and Abu 'Abdullah (peace be upon them) that this applies when a husband and wife quarrel and cannot agree, and their quarrel is prolonged, so they go before the ruler. He sends an arbitrator from the husband's family and an arbitrator from the wife's family, if they are suited to reconcile between them. Even if they cannot resolve their difference, they do not have the right to separate them until the man and the woman both call for a separation — and he should not separate them otherwise.

Mention of al-Ilaa':

Allah the Almighty said: "For those who swear that they will not approach their wives, there is a waiting period of four months. Then if they return, Allah is Forgiving, Merciful. And if they resolve upon divorce, then indeed Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing." (122)

We have narrated from the Imams of the House of the Messenger (peace be upon him and his family) and the Imams of his household that al-Ilaa' is when a man swears to his wife that he will not have intercourse with her and then abandons her for four months or more. Abu 'Abdullah Ja'far ibn Muhammad (peace be upon him) said: "It is not permissible for a woman to stand against

(121) Al-Nisa': 35
(122) Al-Baqarah: 226–227

<!-- page 177 -->
her husband as long as she is under his guardianship until four months have passed from the time of the oath. Four months and beyond — she may stand against him whenever she wishes after four months, so she takes the matter to the Sultan. If she does that, it is better for him to fulfill, meaning to return to her and have intercourse with her in the proper manner, or to divorce her. He must do one of two things." He said: "Even if he abandons her without an oath and wants to abandon her, this is not a divorce and it is not counted as a divorce until the woman takes the matter before the Sultan. He must either fulfill or divorce in the proper manner, and fulfillment is only in a state of purity in which he has not touched her, and with witnesses — as is the case with divorce. The divorce does not apply except to one who has consummated the marriage, and it does not fall upon one with whom he has not been able to be intimate."

It is not permissible for a man to subject himself to al-Ilaa' except with his wife, and he should not be separated from her for four months. Without them, if four months have passed and she stands against him, it is not permissible for him except to fulfill and have intercourse with her properly, or to divorce her. If he is unable to have intercourse due to illness or travel and he acknowledges this with his tongue, that is sufficient. Thus if he is acknowledged as being faithful with his tongue and witnesses testify to it, it is permissible.

From 'Ali (peace be upon him) that he said: "If the husband has sworn al-Ilaa' and decides to divorce, let her remain until she menstruates and then purifies herself, then if she has purified herself he divorces her with a witness of just character. He is more entitled to return to her unless it has reached three utterances. If it is over, he speaks from his own position and she owns herself. If he wishes and she wishes, he may marry her, and he retains what remains of her divorce count."

A man asked Abu Ja'far (peace be upon him), and he was asked: "My wife gave birth to a slave child and I feared that she would ascribe the child to someone else, so I swore by Allah that I would not approach her until she weaned the child." He said (peace be upon him): "The slave is not his wife's doing. It is necessary to fulfill — leave it two months and then stop, and do not go beyond the limit."

He meant (peace be upon him) that whoever swears to his wife that he will not approach her for a month or less than four months, that does not constitute al-Ilaa'. If he does approach her before four months, then it is not an al-Ilaa' — for al-Ilaa' is only with an oath, and fulfillment is an oath.

It was narrated from Ja'far ibn Muhammad (peace be upon him) that he said: "If the husband says, 'I have taken such a vow,' meaning with his right hand — that is if he made the oath before that." For if a man has already sworn al-Ilaa' against his wife and then becomes incapacitated, no separation is made between them, even if he is incapacitated and has not been intimate with her, then he has not been intimate with her.

<!-- page 178 -->
There is no separation between them, and we will mention this in its proper place. He said: "And if he swears for more than four months, he is a master."

Mention of al-Dhihar:

Allah the Almighty said: "Those among you who pronounce al-dhihar to their wives..." to the end of the verse. (123)

It was narrated to us from the Imams of the House of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) that a man came to him and said: "O Messenger of Allah, I have pronounced dhihar against my wife." He said: "Go and free a slave." He said: "I do not have one." He said: "Then fast for two consecutive months." He said: "I cannot." He said: "Then go and feed sixty poor people."

He said (peace be upon him): "If there is something by which one can free from the consequences of dhihar, it is not permissible to fast if he is able to free a slave. If he is able to fast, he fasts, and feeding is not permitted for him. If he finds nothing and cannot fast, then Abu 'Abdullah Ja'far ibn Muhammad (peace be upon him) said: 'Everything in the Qur'an where there is an "or" with choice, he may do whichever he wishes.'" This is like the saying: "Allah will not hold you accountable for what is unintentional in your oaths, but He will hold you accountable for what your hearts have earned" — so the expiation is: feeding ten of the poor from the average of what you feed your own family, or clothing them, or freeing a slave. (124) The unbeliever may choose whichever he wishes, and what is said — "if he does not find such-and-such, or is unable" — similarly the second option is not permitted until the first is found possible.

He said: "Al-dhihar is only valid in a state of purity in which he has not touched her, and with the testimony of two witnesses — as is the case for divorce. If otherwise, it counts for nothing. Al-dhihar does not apply in the case of the right hand, just as divorce does not." Similarly, if the man intends it and says to her in a state of purity, in the presence of just witnesses: "You are to me as my mother's back" — and he does not add "if I do such-and-such." Al-dhihar does not occur more than once in a single sitting — one expiation suffices for it even if he repeats the pronouncement multiple times. But if he pronounces dhihar at separate times, there is expiation for each time. And if he pronounces dhihar against four women, there is expiation for each of them. Al-dhihar is obligatory upon every mahram — such as when he says, "You are like my mother's back."

(123) Al-Mujaddalah: 2
(124) Al-Ma'idah: 89


<!-- page 179 -->
A Book Brief on the Effects — Volume Two

My sister, or aunt, or whoever he addresses with a term of unmarriageable kinship — zihar is therefore obligatory upon him. The zihar remains in effect until he has intercourse with her. There is no zihar between a man and his slave-woman, but if he wishes to approach her before expiating, he must first make expiation. Her situation prior to that expiation has been wronged, and he must seek forgiveness from Allah and repent to Him afterward. If he divorces her and the zihar was pronounced before consummation, no expiation is required of him. If he returns to her after divorcing her, he must not approach her until he has performed the expiation for zihar, even if she has completed her waiting period and he has remarried her — since she had already become clear of him, no new zihar arises from the remarriage.

A slave is obliged to perform the same expiation if his wife is similarly bound to him, yet he is not required to free a slave or feed the poor except where his master donates that to him. If his master does not donate it, he fasts for one month — half of what a free person fasts. The fast of zihar for a free person consists of two consecutive months, as Allah the Almighty has commanded. If he fasts a month or less and then breaks his fast at the beginning of the fasting period, what he fasted does not count. However, if he fasts a month and one or two days — or more — into the second month, and then breaks his fast before completing it, he must make up what remains, because continuity between the two months is required. The freeing of a slave as expiation for zihar may be of a Muslim or a non-Muslim, male or female, because Allah the Almighty said: "So a slave is to be freed," without stipulating conditions. It is not permissible in zihar to free a slave who is insane, defective, corrupt, or committed to management and service in his affairs.

It was narrated from Ali (peace be upon him) that he said: If he cannot find the means for expiation, he should not fast; then if he is freed from his fast before it is over, the fast is broken. Even if he completes his fast and then finds that he has nothing to his name, nothing is required of him. It was also narrated from him that he said: He feeds sixty poor persons, each poor person receiving half a sa'. Ja'far ibn Muhammad (peace be upon him) said that he feeds each poor person. What he stated regarding the prayers of Allah upon him is what he is commanded to do for one who is able. As for one who is unable, what Ja'far ibn Muhammad (peace be upon him) said applies, and his entry is rewarded by the latitude of the expansion. What he said is what is commanded: whoever feeds a poor person in portions. There is no zihar or giving for expiation except when there has been prior intercourse with her, and that applies even if he has not had intercourse with even one of his wives. If he expiates without first doing so, he has sinned and committed a grave wrong, so he must seek forgiveness from Allah and repent to Him.

<!-- page 180 -->
A Book Brief on the Effects — Volume Two

**Mention of Li'an:**

Allah says: "And those who accuse their wives and have no witnesses except themselves — then the testimony of one of them is to testify four times by Allah that he is of the truthful, and the fifth that the curse of Allah be upon him if he is of the liars. And it repels the punishment from her that she testify four times by Allah that he is of the liars, and the fifth that the wrath of Allah be upon her if he is of the truthful. And if it were not for the grace of Allah upon you and His mercy, and that Allah is Wise." (125)

He said: Whoever accuses his wife, there is no li'an between him and her until he claims to have witnessed her himself, saying: "I saw a man between her legs committing adultery with her," or he disavows her child, saying: "This child is not mine." This is only enacted before a judge. If it is established as true and he has not retracted his claim and she has denied what he said, the judge orders li'an. The husband says: "I bear witness by Allah that I have seen a man commit adultery with her," or he says: "I bear witness that this child is not from me" — he says that four times, then says in the fifth: "And the curse of Allah be upon me if I am among the liars." The judge then says "Amen." Thereupon the judge asks the woman. If she acknowledges what he said, she is stoned. If she denies it, she says: "I bear witness by Allah that he is among the liars in what he has accused me of" — saying that four times — then says in the fifth: "And the wrath of Allah be upon me if he is among the truthful." The judge says "Amen." When she has done that, the judge separates between them permanently, and she is never again lawful to him. The child is not attributed to the one who disavowed him and is not ascribed to him; rather the child is attributed to his mother and her relatives, and his inheritance belongs to them. The woman's custody belongs to the one who performed li'an against her.

Li'an is only carried out before a judge. There is no li'an between a man and his wife until he has had intercourse with her, even if he accuses her of adultery or disavows her child. If he refuses to perform li'an and she brings the matter before the Sultan, the hadd punishment for slander is applied against him unless he produces proof of what he said. Even if he has evidence witnessed by four people testifying to adultery against her, li'an is also permissible for the disavowal of her child. He does not perform li'an for the punishment if she raises it before the Sultan, and his only recourse is divorce from her. If he performs li'an and then retracts what he said and declares himself a liar, the hadd is not applied to him, and the child inherits from him, though he does not inherit anything from the child. Li'an is enacted between a Muslim and a dhimmiyyah, between a free man and a slave-woman, between a possessed man and his wife, and between every married couple.

<!-- page 181 -->
Volume Two — A Book Brief on the Effects

Both must be of age, and there is no li'an between two minors until they reach maturity. Furthermore, if they do reach maturity, he cannot perform li'an against her for what he accused his wife of while they were minors. There is no li'an between a mute man and a mute woman, for li'an is enacted only with the tongue. If he accuses his wife when she is silent, they are to be separated and li'an cannot be performed while she is pregnant — it must wait until she gives birth. If a man slanders his wife through an intermediary, there is no li'an for that. If he says to her, "I did not find you a virgin," there is no issue with that, and li'an does not apply, because a woman's virginity may be lost without any prohibited act.

If he accused her before the hadd of slander was applied to him and she is his wife, the two parties performing li'an must stand — they are not to remain seated. If a man accuses his wife and then divorces her and she brings the matter before him while she is still in her 'iddah, li'an is performed. If she brings the matter after her 'iddah has expired, the hadd punishment is applied against him — unless he produces proof of what he said. If he makes the accusation and she dies before li'an is performed, a guardian from her relatives may rise and perform li'an on her behalf, though he does not inherit from her if he does not do so. If a man divorces his wife before consummation and she then claims to be pregnant by him and he denies it — if he clearly loosened her, she performs li'an; otherwise he swears that he has not cohabited with her, and her claim is not accepted. This is all the opinion of the Ahl al-Bayt (peace be upon them).

**Mentioning the 'Iddah, Its Maintenance, and Mourning:**

Allah the Almighty said: "And those of you who die and leave behind wives shall wait for four months and ten nights." (126) He said: "And divorced women shall wait for three menstrual cycles." (127) He said: "And those who are pregnant — their term is when they give birth to their child." (128) And He said: "If you have divorced them before you have touched them." (129) If a man dies leaving a wife, she must observe a waiting period of four months and ten nights, whether he had consummated the marriage with her or not, whether she is an adult or a child, because Allah the Almighty said: "And those of you who die and leave behind wives shall wait for four months and ten nights." (130) No exception is made in this regard — whether consummation took place or not, whether she is a child or an adult.

(126) Al-Baqarah: 234
(127) Al-Baqarah: 228
(128) Al-Talaq: 4
(129) Al-Ahzab: 49
(130) Al-Baqarah: 234

<!-- page 182 -->
Volume Two — A Book Brief on the Effects

If a man divorces his wife while she is menstruating, her 'iddah consists of three complete menstrual cycles and she counts it only while in her husband's house. If a pregnant woman's husband dies, she observes the longer of the two terms: if she gives birth before four months and ten nights have elapsed, she waits the full four months and ten nights; if she has completed the four months and ten nights but has not yet given birth, she waits patiently until she gives birth, because Allah the Almighty said: "And those who are pregnant — their term is when they give birth to their child." (131) And He said: "And those of you who die and whose wives are pregnant..." (132) No exception is made in this. If he divorces her while she is pregnant, her term is when she gives birth. Everything she delivers that is recognized as a child — even if her womb contains two or more — counts, and her 'iddah is not complete until she delivers the last child in her womb.

Whoever divorces his wife with one or two revocable divorces and then dies from her — she observes the 'iddah of death from him, because she did not become free from him and he died while she was still in his bond. Allah the Almighty said: "And those of you who die and leave behind wives shall wait for four months and ten nights." (133) Whoever divorces his wife while he is absent from her — her 'iddah is counted from the day he pronounced the divorce. As for the 'iddah of death for widows, even if he was absent, it is counted from the time the news of his death reached her, and what came before that is not counted, because mourning is obligatory upon her whereas the divorced woman has no mourning.

If a man divorces his wife before consummating the marriage, there is no 'iddah for her and she may marry whenever she wishes. The divorced woman counts her 'iddah based on the onset of menstruation. We have mentioned that the blood of menstruation is distinguished from the blood of istihada: menstrual blood is thick, malodorous, and warm, while the blood of istihada is thin, pure, neither warm nor malodorous. If a woman sees blood of istihada, she follows her established habit, but if she is unaware of this and does not know how to distinguish it, she observes the months.

If a woman is divorced and she has menstruated once or twice, then her menstruation ceases, she resumes counting her 'iddah in months, and the past menstrual cycles do not count — because Allah the Almighty has made it obligatory that three complete cycles be observed by the one who menstruates, while the one who has despaired of menstruation waits three months. Since she did not complete three cycles and then despaired of menstruation, she must wait three months. If her menstruation returns before the completion of three months, she resumes

(131) Al-Talaq: 4
(132) Al-Baqarah: 234
(133) Al-Baqarah: 234

<!-- page 183 -->
A Book Brief on the Effects — Volume Two

counting in menstrual cycles. As for the slave-woman, her 'iddah is two menstrual cycles if she menstruates, or one month and a half if she does not. He may divorce her with two divorces, because divorce is not divisible — it consists of a full divorce and half, just as menstruation is so divided. Her 'iddah from the death of her husband is two months and five days, while the free woman counts a full number of days as she does from among the free.

As for the pregnant woman who is divorced — she is entitled to maintenance and accommodation. The woman who is pregnant in the case of death, however, has no maintenance. The divorced woman during her 'iddah following a revocable divorce is entitled to maintenance and accommodation until her waiting period ends. The irrevocably divorced woman has no maintenance and no accommodation. Mourning is for the widow whose husband has died — not for the divorced woman, nor for the woman separated by khul', nor for the woman separated by li'an. Mourning means that the widow does not comb her hair during the period she is counting, does not dye it, does not apply perfume, does not adorn herself, and does not wear a dyed garment — except one that is black. She does not leave her house during the day nor remain away from her house overnight. If she wishes, she may go out at the end of the night and return before morning — this is the essence of mourning.

**Mentioning the Mut'a of Divorce and the Substitution of Divorce:**

Allah the Almighty said: "There is no blame upon you if you divorce women before you have touched them or specified for them an obligation. And provide for them — upon the wealthy according to his means and upon the poor according to his means — a provision according to what is right, a duty upon the doers of good." (134) And enjoyment is obligatory. He said: "And for divorced women there is a provision according to what is right — a duty upon the righteous." (135)

Mut'a applies to a divorced woman according to the Sunnah and to the woman in her 'iddah, whether he consummated the marriage with her or not. There is no mut'a for the woman from whom a dowry has been determined or for the woman from whom mahr has been established, and there is no fixed limit on the amount of mut'a, as Allah the Almighty said: "According to his means and according to his means." The provision of mut'a for one with means is recommended to be generous — with dirhams, dinars, a slave, a slave-girl, goods, or food — all of which has been narrated from the Imams (peace of Allah be upon them). The mut'a is not given until after the 'iddah has ended and she has become free from him, whether it lasts that long or not. If a man divorces his wife in the manner we have described — according to the 'iddah or the Sunnah — three divorces are not permissible for him, as Allah the Almighty said, except after another husband has married her and consummated the marriage.

(134) Al-Baqarah: 236
(135) Al-Baqarah: 241


<!-- page 184 -->
Then he divorces her, or dies, and the first husband may remarry her — if he wishes — after her waiting period from the second husband has expired. This constitutes a resumption of marriage, and she retains the remaining count of her divorces. If he then divorces her three times and she marries another, she does not become lawful to him again after the three divorces, even if she remarries, unless that marriage is to a willing adult man who consummates it with her.

It is lawful for him to have a slave woman whom he has entered after she has reached puberty, and this does not dissolve the marriage for him. Marriage is a right, and it is not permissible for a man to collude with another to marry her in order to make her lawful for himself, nor to arrange for someone else to do so. If he divorces her once or twice, and she then marries another who either dies or divorces her, and the first husband remarries her after her waiting period from the second husband has expired, she retains whatever divorces remained to her. This is the exception of "except after a husband," and this is the position of the Ahl al-Bayt, may Allah's prayers be upon them.

**On the Return, Slaves, and What Is Obligatory Upon Husbands Regarding Dowries:**

We have mentioned above, in describing divorce, that if a man divorces his wife he may take her back, unless she has concluded her waiting period. If her waiting period has concluded and he wishes to marry her again and she consents, this constitutes a resumption of marriage by mutual agreement, and she remains with him for what is left of her divorce count. If he reviews her before her waiting period expires, he may do so whether she consents or not, and she remains with him for what is left of her divorce count. The return must be witnessed by two just witnesses. If he reviews her, he may do so without compensation or a new dowry, provided he has not concluded her waiting period. If her waiting period has passed and they agree to return, he may not take her back except with a new named dowry.

A man does not require his wife's permission for the return so long as she has a return remaining. If he divorces her a third time, there is no return to her. The divorce of a slave from a free woman counts as three, and the divorce of a free man from a slave woman counts as two. If a man divorces his wife before intercourse, she receives half the dowry, as Allah said. If he had given her an animal as dowry and it reproduced before he divorced her prior to consummation, he takes back half of the animal and half of its offspring. If the animal is pregnant when he takes it back, half of it belongs to her. If he had given her a dowry of silver and she returned it to him, or gave it as charity to him, and he then divorced her before consummating the marriage, he returns half of it to her — even if she was his slave woman whom he had freed and made her freedom her dowry, as we mentioned before, and he then divorced her before consummating the marriage.

<!-- page 185 -->
He returns to her half of her value as assessed on the day he married her. If he dies without having given her a dowry, there is no claim for her, and she inherits from him but has no dowry.

<!-- page 186 -->

<!-- page 187 -->
**Doors of Manumission**

<!-- page 188 -->

<!-- page 189 -->
**On Manumission:**

Allah Almighty said: "But he has not broken through the difficult pass — and what will make you know what the difficult pass is?" (136)

The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said: "Whoever frees a Muslim soul — a believer — Allah will protect each of her limbs from the Fire." It is narrated from ʿAlī, may Allah's prayers be upon him, that he freed a thousand slaves, all of them purchased from the earnings of his own hand. From Jaʿfar ibn Muḥammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, it is narrated: "There are four things for which Allah will, by His will, make Paradise obligatory: feeding a hungry liver, clothing a bare body, freeing a captive neck, and the best is the believing neck. The most self-sufficient among them, then the believing woman who stands on her own, then the Muslim man, then the Muslim woman. The best of them are the most expensive and the most cherished in the eyes of their families. The young who has nothing to spare for himself until he reaches adulthood is the most virtuous, and it is permissible to free one who does not share the religion of Islam."

It was narrated from ʿAlī, may Allah's prayers and peace be upon him, that he freed a Christian slave and accepted his manumission even though the man had not become Muslim. Manumission is only for the sake of Allah Almighty. The Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him, said: "There is no emancipation except that which is sought for the face of Allah." There is no emancipation in an oath when the one swearing swears by freeing his slave or slaves, for he has not committed perjury thereby, since an oath is only binding in the name of Allah Almighty. What is in the hands of others does not compel a man to free his slave or slaves; if he does free them it is disliked unless they have not yet been freed.

If a man frees some of his servants at the time of death, or during an illness from which he subsequently dies, all of it is manumission for him, provided it does not exceed one-third of the estate. The heirs are not permitted to free beyond what has been specified unless they are children. What is within one-third is freed; what exceeds one-third remains. For example, if the value of the slave is ten dinars and the estate is twenty dinars, other than the slave whose partial freedom was stipulated, he is freed because one-third covers him. If his value is ten dinars and the estate is five dinars, only half of him is freed, because one-third of the estate is five dinars — which is half his value. This calculation applies proportionally whether greater or lesser. If the heirs permit manumission, and those whose permission is valid consent, the slave is freed in his entirety according to their shares after the portion allotted to the one whose Lord freed him.

(136) Al-Balad: 11–13

<!-- page 190 -->
If a man frees part of his slave while in good health, the whole of that slave is freed, whether he has wealth or not, for a human being cannot be divided. There is no partnership with Allah Almighty in what has been mentioned, and if he shares in a slave and frees his portion of it — along with whatever remained — there is no partnership if he had the means to do so. He frees all of him even if he is poor and has nothing but what he has freed from that slave.

If a man frees a slave or slaves while ill from the illness from which he dies, and he has no other wealth, and the heirs have not given their permission for manumission, then only one-third of them may be freed. If he named them one after another at the time of death, the first named is the first freed, proceeding through their names until one-third of their value is reached. If reaching one-third of their value is not completed, the remaining ones — whose names were recorded after the completion of the one-third — are not freed. If, however, he named them all together at once, saying "My servants are free for the face of Allah" or "These are free," they are to be divided into thirds, even if one person cannot be divided into thirds — one-third of each of them is freed. If he freed them while burdened by a debt that consumes their price, the manumission is not permitted. Likewise, it is not permissible if the debt exceeds half their value. If the debt is equal to half their value or less, they are retained pending the debt and the heirs' claim; if the debt is paid, they are freed. One of them should not be enslaved if the lots determined otherwise. The lots are six, so if one-sixth of a slave is freed by will, or more than that, he is not enslaved.

Whoever frees a slave on a condition that is lawful — and the condition is lawful — the condition is binding upon the slave and the manumission is valid. Whoever possesses someone upon whom mercy is obligatory is freed from him when he comes to own him as he wishes, or a father who has no means of slavery over him since kinship forbids that one own in his house one with whom one cannot lawfully marry, unless one of them is a woman whose marriage to the other is not permissible, and no otherwise. It is permissible for them to be related if the ownership is through a lawful means.

**On the Kitāba:**

Allah Almighty said: "And those of your slaves who seek a writing — write it for them if you know good in them." (137) Whoever wishes his servant to draw up a kitāba contract may do so, though it is not obligatory upon the master to comply. (137) Al-Nūr: 33

<!-- page 191 -->
The Almighty commanded it as a matter of devotion and encouragement, not as a binding obligation. It applies to all people of the qibla and otherwise — every slave who has good in him may ask his master to draw up the kitāba for him, and it displeases the master to refuse if the slave is capable. He is free to do it or not. The "good" here is the capacity to earn and uprightness, not wealth, as Allah Almighty said: "It is prescribed for you that when one of you faces death, if he leaves wealth behind..." — meaning money — because a slave has no money; his money belongs to his master. Nor is it obligatory for the master to write the kitāba for a servant who has neither a profession nor money. If he draws up the kitāba for a sum of money, he names one installment or multiple installments for a fixed period, provided the slave can meet it. It is permissible to draw it up in this way, and upon fulfilling its terms the slave is freed by virtue of the decree and its rulings. He performs the rulings of the free with respect to what has been fulfilled, and retains the rulings of the slave with respect to what remains. If the master stipulates in the kitāba contract that if the slave is unable to pay one installment or all installments — if there is only one — at the time it falls due, he reverts to slavery, and the master takes what he received from him, then the condition is valid. Nothing of the slave is freed except upon fulfilling the final obligation upon him, and he remains a slave in his status during that time. He is not adjudged incapacitated if the installments are two or more. The children born to a maktūba during the period of the kitāba are freed along with her when she is freed, as are the children born to her nation from others. This is the position of the Ahl al-Bayt, may Allah's prayers be upon them.

**On the Mudabbar and the Mothers of Children:**

The mudabbar arrangement is the management by which the master says: "If I die, my servant so-and-so — whether male servant, female servant, or mother of my child — shall be free." If he says this, he may use his slaves during his lifetime, place conditions upon his mudabbar, and employ them. When his mudabbar bears a child from another, that child takes her status and is freed along with her. When the master dies, they are freed from his estate — from one-third of it — unless the heirs permit their emancipation from beyond one-third. If they do not fall within one-third, they are not freed from it, in the manner we have described for manumission during illness. If he retracts his plan before he dies, they remain as servants.

(138) Al-Baqarah: 180


<!-- page 192 -->

Ownership and management constitute a form of bequest, and the testator has the right to revoke any of his bequests at will until he dies. Whoever designates his pregnant slave-woman as a mother of his child without excluding her son is thereby confirmed in that position, and she is freed and her status established. If the master wishes to free her and return to managing his slave-woman's affairs, that is permitted for him, and the rulings applicable to the mother of the child apply to her. The rulings governing a slave apply to her as well, except that she may not be sold except for the price of her neck — that is, if her master purchased her but had not yet paid for her, then she gave birth to his child, and the seller pressed him for payment and he had no money; or if he freed her while in that state and the freeing was not permitted. The mother of the child is not to be sold except for the price of her neck. Likewise, if he purchased her while wealthy and did not pay the price until he went bankrupt, and she then became a mother of his child — she is not to be sold for his debts, nor even for the price of her neck. When the one who fathered her child dies, she is freed by his death, having borne him a child from a pregnancy he acknowledged, whether the child was complete or incomplete, born alive or dead. Loyalty of the mother of the child belongs to the one whose child she bore and then to whoever assumes his right after him. Loyalty of a freed person belongs to the one who freed him, except when the freed person is released unconditionally — that is, when one says upon freeing him: "I have freed you for the sake of God, and your lineage is your own; no loyalty is owed to me or to anyone." If the liberator says this, no bond of loyalty attaches to him. If he does not say it, loyalty to him remains and is not given away. Loyalty passes after the death of the freed person to those who inherit from him by kinship — male and female alike — to the extent of their shares of inheritance. Loyalty is not inherited by spouses, nor do spouses inherit anything from the bond of loyalty, nor does anything of loyalty pass to them. What remains after the designated heirs from inheritance passes by kinship and by possession. The woman's loyalty belongs to the one who freed her and thereafter to her heirs according to what has been described. If a slave marries a free woman, her children are free and belong to the one to whom their mother belongs. If their father is freed, his loyalty passes to the one who freed their father. Loyalty belongs to the ancestor because he freed the man who was a slave. Thus if the freed man dies leaving two children, then one of those children dies leaving a son, and then the master who first freed the father dies with no heir other than his freed client — then the inheritance goes to the son of the one freed, not to the son's son, for this is the meaning of loyalty belonging to the ancestor. This is the position of the People of the House — may God's blessings be upon them all.

<!-- page 193 -->

On Gifts and Generosity

Gifts, good works, and beneficence for the sake of God Almighty and in pursuit of His reward encompass virtue, righteousness, and recompense. The Messenger of God — peace be upon him and his family — said: "All of creation are the children of God, and the most beloved of creation to God Almighty is the one who benefits His children, who brings joy to a household, and who walks with a Muslim brother in his time of need." He said that such a person is more beloved to God Almighty than two months of i'tikaf in the Sacred Mosque. He also said — prayers be upon him — that God blesses the people of goodness by making them a means of fulfilling needs for those who seek their help. This is because through them comes praise, reward, and remembrance from God. Whoever initiates a good deed for one of God's creatures — Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali, peace be upon him, said: "If God raises a believer from his grave, he emerges bearing a good example, and when he passes through these trials, it says to him, 'Do not fear and do not grieve,' and it continues to reassure him and give him glad tidings until it returns him to God, and He brings him to account swiftly, then commands him to Paradise. The believer asks it, 'Who are you? May God have mercy on you — you promised me and kept your word and believed me.' It says, 'I am the joy that you used to bring to believers. Today I will take you.'" Ja'far ibn Muhammad, peace be upon him, said: "There is nothing more excellent than beneficence, and what makes it excellent is that it is guidance from God Almighty to His believing servant. Not everyone who wishes to do good to people is opened to it, and not everyone who wishes to do it is able. Whoever is able to do it has been granted permission, for God Almighty has gathered for him the desire to do good, the ability to do it, and the permission to do it — and that is a happiness and an honor for the one who seeks it." He said: "Beneficence is only properly done with three qualities: making little of it, making it easy, and hastening it. If it is made little of, it becomes great in the eyes of the one to whom it is given; if it is made pleasant, it is rendered complete for him; and if it is hastened, it becomes a source of congratulation. If these things are not done, it is as though the gift were denied." He said, peace be upon him: "The best of Muslims is the one who arrives, helps, and provides." He also said, peace be upon him: "I heard my father, peace be upon him, say that the initiation of good deeds wards off the blow of evil." All good deeds are a form of charity. The people of goodness in this world are the people of goodness in the hereafter, and they are the first to enter Paradise. He said: "The Messenger of God — peace be upon him — said: 'Whoever is led to a favor, let him reward it; if he is unable, let him acknowledge it. If he does not, he has been ungrateful for the blessing.'" We have narrated from the Imams of the People of the House of the Messenger of God —

<!-- page 194 -->

may God's prayers be upon them — that they permitted a man to favor some of his children over others in his wealth and gifts if he wished. A gift or charity is valid whether the recipient accepts it willingly or not. Whoever gives something as a gift for the sake of God Almighty, or out of mercy or kinship, has no right to take it back. If he is given a gift or gives a gift in exchange for compensation, that is disliked, and he may take it back if no compensation is given. Whoever gives a gift or charity to someone with whom he shares a womb or kinship, and that person dies and the gift returns to the giver through inheritance, that is permissible.

The 'umra, the 'aqb, and the sukna — which are forms of virtue and reward — are defined as follows: the 'umra is to give to a man a house, an estate, or something from which he may benefit for his lifetime. The meaning of the condition in it is that whichever of them dies first, the property returns to the one who gave the 'umra or to his heirs, even if that was the arrangement. If the man stipulates that it is for his own lifetime, and he dies, it returns to his heirs. If the condition is for the lifetime of the recipient, it belongs to the recipient for his life, and if the giver dies, his heirs have no claim to take it from the recipient's possession — he may dwell in it and live in it until he dies. If it is made for the recipient and his heirs, it belongs to him and to them after him, as the giver has made it. Gifts are recommended when they are for the sake of God Almighty, and there is no good in what is given to make a false claim, or to seek compensation for something like it, or to fulfill a need, or to take care of someone whose support is obligatory upon his brother. It is the duty of a man's brother to provide for the needs of a believer and to take care of him in the right way. If the believer accepts something in lieu of that, he is among those who testify, and likewise is what he takes for the sake of his brother. What was manufactured in falsehood and what was obligatory — such as zakat and the like — the virtue is that one gives it openly and announces it. As for voluntary charity, it is good to give it quietly; but if announced, that is also acceptable. God Almighty said: "If you show charity, it is a blessing; but if you conceal it and give it to the poor, it is better for you."(139) All righteous acts of obligation are better performed openly and publicly, while their supererogatory counterparts and secrets are better concealed. Thus the obligatory prayer in mosques and congregations is better, and performing nafl prayers openly is also preferable. Yet if a man declares that he has prayed Dhuhr or 'Asr or any obligatory prayer, that is not permissible for him to say. Similarly, if he says that he has fasted Ramadan, or that he has stood in night prayer, or that he has fasted such-and-such a voluntary fast — that declaration is

(139) Al-Baqarah: 271
